[
91
, Chapter 7]
.

2.
Gauge theory preliminaries

Throughout the paper, we will fix

•

\Sigma
a connected closed
oriented topological surface of genus
g\geq 2
;

•

\mathsf{G}
a connected complex semisimple Lie group with center
Z(\mathsf{G})
, Lie
algebra
\mathfrak{g}
,
and Killing form
\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}
; and

•

\pi_{P}:P\to\Sigma
a smooth right principal
\mathsf{G}
-bundle.

2.1.
Principal bundles

2.1.1.
Basic definitions

The
vertical bundle
of
P
is the distribution
\mathrm{V}P
defined by
\mathrm{V}P=\ker(d\pi_{P})
. It fits into the exact sequence

(2.1)

0\longrightarrow\mathrm{V}P\longrightarrow\mathrm{T}P\longrightarrow\pi_{P}^{*}(\mathrm{T}\Sigma)\longrightarrow 0~.

Since the right
\mathsf{G}
-action preserves the vertical bundle, every element
X\in\mathfrak{g}
in the Lie algebra spans a vertical vector field that we denote by
X^{\sharp}
.

Given a linear representation
\rho:\mathsf{G}\to\mathsf{GL}(W)
, the
associated bundle
is defined by

P(W):=\mathsf{G}\backslash(P\times W)~,

where
g\cdotp(p,v)=(pg^{-1},\rho(g)v)
. The projection on the first factor turns
P(W)
into a vector bundle over
\Sigma
.
The main example is
P(\mathfrak{g})
, where
\rho
is the adjoint representation.
We will denote the space of
P(W)
-valued
k
-forms on
\Sigma
by
\Omega^{k}(\Sigma,P(W))
.

Denote the space of
W
-valued
k
-forms on
P
by
\Omega^{k}(P,W)
, and let
\Omega^{k}(P,W)^{\mathsf{G}}
denote the subset of forms which are equivariant with respect to the natural
\mathsf{G}
-action.
A vector field
V
on
P
defines a contraction map

\iota_{V}:\Omega^{k}(P,W)\to\Omega^{k-1}(P,W)
.
A
W
-valued
k
-form
\psi\in\Omega^{k}(P,W)
is called
basic

if it is equivariant and
\iota_{V}(\psi)=0
for all vertical vector fields
V\in\mathrm{V}P
. Denote the spaces of basic
W
-valued k-forms by
\Omega_{b}^{k}(P,W)
.

Given a basic form
\hat{\psi}\in\Omega^{k}_{b}(P,W)
, define
\psi\in\Omega^{k}(\Sigma,P(W))
by

\psi_{x}(u_{1},...,u_{k}):=\left[\left(p,\hat{\psi}_{p}(\hat{u}_{1},...,\hat{u}_{k})\right)\right]~,

where
x\in\Sigma
,
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\in\mathrm{T}_{x}\Sigma,
and
p,\hat{u}_{1},\dots,\hat{u}_{k}
are lifts of
x,u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
, respectively. One checks that this is independent of the choices of lifts, and defines a isomorphism
\Omega^{k}_{b}(P,W)\cong\Omega^{k}(\Sigma,P(W))
between basic W-valued
k
-forms on
P
and k-forms valued in
P(W)
.

There is a natural bracket

[\cdot,\cdot]:\Omega^{k}(P,\mathfrak{g})\times\Omega^{\ell}(P,\mathfrak{g})\to\Omega^{k+\ell}(P,\mathfrak{g})

defined by

[\zeta,\eta](V_{1},\cdots,V_{k+\ell})=[\zeta(V_{1},\cdots,V_{k}),\eta(V_{k+1},\cdots,V_{k+\ell})].

Note that
[\zeta,\eta]=(-1)^{k\ell+1}[\eta,\zeta]
, and that
[\zeta,\eta]
is basic whenever
\zeta
and
\eta
are basic.
This defines a bracket on
P(\mathfrak{g})
-valued forms on
\Sigma
which we also denote by
[\cdot,\cdot].

2.1.2.
Connections on principal bundles and associated bundles

There are many ways to think about connections on principal bundles, we will use the following equivalent definitions.

Definition 2.1
.

The following are equivalent definitions of a connection
P:

(1)

A
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant
1
-form
B\in\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{\mathsf{G}}
such that
B(x^{\sharp})=x
for any
x\in\mathfrak{g}
.

(2)

A
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant projection
B:\mathrm{T}P\to\mathrm{V}P
.

(3)

A
\mathsf{G}
-invariant splitting of (
2.1
).

Denote the space of connections on
P
by
\mathcal{A}(P)
.

By (3),
a connection
B
defines a splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}
, where the distribution
\mathrm{H}_{B}
is defined by the kernel of
B
and is called the
horizontal distribution
of
B
.
Since the difference between two connections on
P
is basic,
\mathcal{A}(P)
is an affine space modeled on
\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
.
Explicitly, given a basic 1-form
\psi\in\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
, in the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}
the connection
B+\psi
is given by

B+\psi=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&\psi\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}:\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}\to\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B},

where we have identified
\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
with
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant bundle maps
\pi_{P}^{*}(\mathrm{T}\Sigma)\cong\mathrm{H}_{B}\to\mathrm{V}P
.

The
curvature
of a connection
B
is the
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
2
-form

F_{B}=dB+\tfrac{1}{2}[B,B]~.

One checks that
F_{B}
is basic and so can be considered as an element in
\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
. Equivalently, the curvature can be defined using the Lie bracket of horizontal vector fields: given
V,W\in\mathrm{H}_{B}
, we have

F_{B}(V,W)=-B([V,W]).

Given a representation
\rho:\mathsf{G}\to\mathsf{GL}(W)
, let
d\rho:\mathfrak{g}\to\End(W)
denote the associated Lie algebra representation. For a connection
B\in\mathcal{A}(P)
, define

(2.2)

d_{B}:\Omega^{k}(P,W)\to\Omega^{k+1}(P,W),

by
d_{B}(\eta)=d\eta+(d\rho\circ B)\wedge\eta.
One checks that
d_{B}
preserves basic forms and hence defines a covariant derivative
d_{B}:\Omega^{k}(\Sigma,P(W))\to\Omega^{k+1}(\Sigma,P(W))
.

2.1.3.
Structure group reductions and metrics

Let
\mathsf{K}<\mathsf{G}
be a maximal compact subgroup of
\mathsf{G}
and

\mathfrak{k}\subset\mathfrak{g}
its Lie algebra. As
\mathfrak{k}
is a real form of

\mathfrak{g}
, it is the fixed point subalgebra of a conjugate linear involution

\tau_{0}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}

called the
Cartan involution
associated to
\mathfrak{k}.

The Killing form
\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}
of
\mathfrak{g}
is negative definite on

\mathfrak{k},
and hence defines a
\mathsf{K}
-invariant hermitian inner
product on
\mathfrak{g}
defined by

\langle x,y\rangle_{\tau_{0}}:=\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(x,-\tau_{0}(y))~.

For
y\in\mathfrak{g},
the hermitian adjoint of
\ad_{y}
is
\ad_{-\tau_{0}(y)}
. Hence we use the notation
-\tau_{0}(y)=y^{*}
.

A
reduction of structure group
of
P
to
\mathsf{K}
is the choice of

\mathsf{K}
-invariant subbundle
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
. Using the canonical
identification between the associated bundles
P_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathfrak{g})
and

P(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathsf{K}
-invariance of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\tau_{0}},
the extensions of

\tau_{0}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\tau_{0}}
to
P(\mathfrak{g})
define a hermitian metric on
P(\mathfrak{g})
.

Equivalently, such a reduction is given by a
Cartan involution on
P
, that is a
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant map
\tau:P\to\End(\mathfrak{g})
(where
\mathsf{G}
acts on
\End(\mathfrak{g})
via conjugation by
\Ad
) such that
\tau(p)
is a Cartan involution for any
p\in P
. The corresponding hermitian metric is then given by

\langle s_{1},s_{2}\rangle_{\tau}(x)=-\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(s_{1}(p),\tau(p)s_{2}(p))

for local sections
s_{a}
of
P(\mathfrak{g})
, regarded as
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant
maps
\sigma_{a}:P\to\mathfrak{g}
for the adjoint action.

The Cartan involution and hermitian metric extend to
P(\mathfrak{g})
-valued
1
-forms as follows.

Definition 2.2
.

Consider
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
of the form
\sigma_{a}=\alpha_{a}\otimes s_{a}

with
\alpha_{a}\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{C})
and
s_{a}\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
.

(1)

The extension of the Cartan involution to
\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
is defined by

\tau(\sigma):=\overline{\alpha}\otimes\tau(s)~.

We use the notation:
\sigma^{*}=-\tau(\sigma)=\overline{\alpha}\otimes s^{*}
.

(2)

The Killing form defines

\displaystyle\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})

\displaystyle:=\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(s_{1},s_{2})\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}~\in\Omega^{0}(T^{\ast}\Sigma\otimes T^{\ast}\Sigma,\mathbb{C})\ ,

\displaystyle\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\sigma_{1}\wedge\sigma_{2})

\displaystyle:=\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(s_{1},s_{2})\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}~\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{C})\ .

(3)

The combination of
\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\tau
defines:

\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\sigma_{1}\wedge\sigma_{2}^{*}):=-\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(s_{1},\tau(s_{2}))\alpha_{1}\wedge\overline{\alpha}_{2}~.

For each Riemann surface structure
X=(\Sigma,j)
on
\Sigma
, the hermitian metric defines an
L^{2}
-inner product on
P(\mathfrak{g})
-valued
(p,q)
-forms.
The following signs are important (see Appendix
A
).

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
are
(p,q)
-forms valued in
P(\mathfrak{g}),
then

\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle:=\int_{X}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(s_{1},s_{2}^{*})\alpha_{1}\wedge\bar{\star}\alpha_{2}=\begin{cases}i\int_{X}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\sigma_{1}\wedge\sigma_{2}^{*})&\text{ if }(p,q)=(1,0)\\
-i\int_{X}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\sigma_{1}\wedge\sigma_{2}^{*})&\text{ if }(p,q)=(0,1)\end{cases}

2.2.
Complex structures on principal bundles

We now describe the space of complex structures on a principal
\mathsf{G}
-bundle.

Definition 2.4
.

A
(principal) complex structure
on
P
is an almost complex
structure
J\in\End(\mathrm{T}P)
satisfying

(1)

J
commutes with the right
\mathsf{G}
-action: for any
g
in
\mathsf{G}
we
have
J\circ(R_{g})_{\ast}=(R_{g})_{\ast}\circ J
.

(2)

For any
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, we have
J(X^{\sharp})=(iX)^{\sharp}
.

We denote by
\mathcal{J}(P)
the space of principal complex structures on
P
.

One checks that a complex structure on
P
is always integrable since the obstruction to integrability of
J
lies in
\Omega^{0,2}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
.
Denote the space of (almost) complex structures on
\Sigma
by
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
. There is a projection map

\pi_{\mathcal{J}}:\mathcal{J}(P)\to\mathcal{J}(\Sigma).

Here
j=\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(J)
is defined by
j_{x}(u)=J_{p}(\hat{u})
for any lift
(p,\hat{u})
of
(x,u)
to
\mathrm{T}P
.
Since
J
preserves the vertical bundle and commutes with the
\mathsf{G}
-action,
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(J)
is independent of the choice of lift.

2.2.1.
The Chern-Singer connection

A connection
B\in\mathcal{A}(P)
is
compatible
with a complex structure
J
if the horizontal distribution of
B
is
J
-invariant. Equivalently,
B
is compatible with
J
if and only if
B
is of type
(1,0)
with respect to
J
, meaning that
B\circ J=iB
.

Given a connection
B\in\mathcal{A}(P)
and a complex structure
j\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, there is a unique complex structure
J\in\pi_{\mathcal{J}}^{-1}(j)
which is compatible with
B
. In the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}
, it is given by

(2.3)

J=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}i&0\\
0&\pi^{*}j\end{array}\right)~.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
be a structure group reduction to a maximal compact subgroup
\mathsf{K}<\mathsf{G}
.

(1)

For each
J\in\mathcal{J}(P)
, there is a unique connection
A_{J}\in\mathcal{A}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
which is compatible with
J
.

(2)

The set of connections on
P
compatible with a complex structure
J\in\mathcal{J}(P)
is an affine space modeled on
\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
, where the type is computed using
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(J)
.

(3)

The projection
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}:\mathcal{J}(P)\to\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
turns
\mathcal{J}(P)
into an affine bundle where the fiber over
j
is modeled on
\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
.

Item (1) is due to Singer
[
85
]
and is the principal bundle analogue of the Chern connection on a holomorphic hermitian vector bundle. We will refer to the isomorphism

(2.4)

\mathrm{S}:\mathcal{J}(P)\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}(P_{\mathsf{K}}),

as the Chern-Singer map, and the
unitary connection
\mathrm{S}(J)=A_{J}
as the
Chern-Singer connection
.
We will use the same notation for the extension of
A_{J}
to a connection on
P
.

Proof.

For item (1), let
J\in\mathcal{J}(P)
. For each
p\in P_{\mathsf{K}}
, consider the
J
-invariant space
H_{p}(J):=\mathrm{T}_{p}P_{\mathsf{K}}\cap J(\mathrm{T}_{p}P_{\mathsf{K}})
. Since
\mathsf{K}
is a real form of
\mathsf{G}
,
H_{p}(J)
intersects trivially the vertical space
\mathrm{V}_{p}P_{\mathsf{K}}
. Furthermore, since
\mathrm{T}_{p}P_{\mathsf{K}}
has co-dimension
\tfrac{1}{2}\dim(\mathsf{G})
, then
H_{p}(J)
has dimension at least
2
. In particular,
\mathrm{T}_{p}P_{K}=\mathrm{V}_{p}P_{K}\oplus H_{p}(J)
and so
\{H_{p}(J)\}_{p\in P_{\mathsf{K}}}
defines a horizontal distribution. Such a distribution is
\mathsf{K}
-invariant because
J
commutes with the
\mathsf{G}
-action.

For item
(2)
, consider a connection
B
compatible with
J
. In the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}
, any other connection
B^{\prime}
is given by

B^{\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{Id}&\beta\\
0&0\end{array}\right)~,

where
\beta\in\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
. The condition
B^{\prime}\circ J=iB^{\prime}
is equivalent to
\beta\circ j=i\beta
for
j=\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(J)
.

For item
(3)
, fix a background connection
B
on
P
. In the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}
, a point
J
in the fiber
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}^{-1}(j)
has the form

J=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}i&\beta\\
0&\pi^{*}j\end{array}\right)~,

for some basic
1
-form
\beta\in\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
. The condition
J^{2}=-\mathrm{Id}
is equivalent to
i\beta+\beta\circ\pi^{*}j=0
. Hence, under the identification
\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})\cong\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
,
\beta
is a
(0,1)
-form with respect to
j
.
∎

2.2.2.
Dolbeault operator on associated bundles

We now explain how
a complex structure
J
on
P
defines a Dolbeault operator on associated bundles. Given a representation
\rho:\mathsf{G}\to\mathsf{GL}(W)
and a connection
B
on
P
compatible with
J
, define

\bar{\partial}_{j}:\Omega^{0}(X,P(V))\longrightarrow\Omega^{0,1}(X,P(V))\ :\ s\longmapsto(d_{B}s)^{0,1}~.

Lemma 2.6
.

The operator
\overline{\partial}_{J}
is a Dolbeault operator on
P(V)
that is independent of the choice of compatible connection
B
.

Proof.

The fact that
\overline{\partial}_{J}
is a Dolbeault operator follows directly from the Leibniz rule for
d_{B}
. The second statement follows from item (2) of Proposition
2.5
. In fact, any other compatible connection differs from
B
by a
(1,0)
-form, hence its
(0,1)
-projection is the same.
∎

3.
Differential geometry of configuration spaces

3.1.
Tangent spaces and complex structures

The tangent space at a complex structure
j\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
is given by

\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)=\{m\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{T}\Sigma)~|~jm+mj=0\}~.

The condition
jm+mj=0
implies that the complex linear extension
m^{\mathbb{C}}
of
m
has the form

m^{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mu\\
\overline{\mu}&0\end{pmatrix}:\mathrm{T}_{j}^{1,0}\Sigma\oplus\mathrm{T}_{j}^{0,1}\Sigma\longrightarrow\mathrm{T}_{j}^{1,0}\Sigma\oplus\mathrm{T}_{j}^{0,1}\Sigma.

The tensor
\mu\in\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{T}_{j}^{1,0}\Sigma)
is called a
Beltrami differential
.

Definition 3.1
.

Post-composing with
j
defines an almost complex structure

\mathrm{I}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{T}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
,

\mathrm{I}_{j}:\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(S)~:~m\longmapsto jm\ .

In terms of Beltrami differentials, the almost complex structure is
\mathrm{I}_{j}(\mu)=i\mu.

Given a complex structure
J\in\mathcal{J}(P)
, let
\Omega^{0}(\End(\mathrm{T}P))^{\mathsf{G}}
denote the space of equivariant sections. The tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)
is given by

\mathrm{T}_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)=\{M\in\Omega^{0}(\End(\mathrm{T}P))^{\mathsf{G}}~|~\text{$M(X)=0$ for all $X\in\Omega^{0}(\mathrm{V}P)$ and }JM+MJ=0\}~.

Let
B\in\mathcal{A}(P)
be a connection which is compatible with
J\in\mathcal{J}(P)
. In the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}
, tangent vectors are given by

(3.1)

M=\begin{pmatrix}0&\beta\\
0&m\end{pmatrix}:\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}\to\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}.

Using (
2.3
), we have

JM+MJ=\begin{pmatrix}0&i\beta+\beta\circ\pi^{*}j\\
0&m\circ\pi^{*}j+\pi^{*}j\circ m\end{pmatrix}.

Since
M
is equivariant,
\beta
is identified with a basic
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
(0,1)
-form
\beta\in\Omega^{0,1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
, and
m
is the pullback of a tangent vector
\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
.

As in Definition
3.1
, we have an almost complex structure
\mathrm{I}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{J}(P)\to\mathrm{T}\mathcal{J}(P)
defined by

(3.2)

\mathrm{I}_{J}:\mathrm{T}_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)\longrightarrow\mathrm{T}_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)~:~M\longmapsto JM\ .

If
B\in\mathcal{A}(P)
is a connection compatible with
J,
then writing
M=(\beta,m)
as in (
3.1
), we have

\mathrm{I}_{J}(\beta,m)=(i\beta,jm)~.

In particular, the projection
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}:\mathcal{J}(P)\to\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
is holomorphic.
The complex structures
\mathrm{I}
and holomorphic projection
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}
extend to the product

\mathcal{J}(P)\times\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g}).

Definition 3.2
.

The
configuration space
of Higgs bundles
\mathcal{C}(P)
is defined by

\mathcal{C}(P)=\{(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{J}(P)\times\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})~|~\Phi\circ J=i\Phi\}.

Denote the restriction of the projection map by

\pi_{\mathcal{J}}:\mathcal{C}(P)\to\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
.

The tangent space of
\mathcal{C}(P)
is given by

\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)=\{(M,\theta)\in\mathrm{T}_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)\times\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})~|~\Phi\circ M+\theta\circ J=i\theta\}~.

Lemma 3.3
.

For each tangent vector
(M,\theta)\in\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
we have

\theta=\psi+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\circ\mu,

where
\psi
has type
(1,0)
with respect to
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(J,\Phi)
and
\mu
is the Beltrami differential associated to
d\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(M,\theta)
.

We will write
\Phi\circ\mu
simply as
\Phi\mu
.

Proof.

The condition
\Phi\circ M+\theta\circ J=i\theta
is equivalent to
\theta^{(0,1)_{J}}=\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\circ M~.
Moreover, since
\Phi
is basic and of type
(1,0)
,
\Phi\circ M=\Phi\mu
, where
\mu
is the associated Beltrami differential.
∎

For
(M,\theta)\in\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
we have

\Phi\circ(JM)+J\theta\circ J=iJ\theta.

Indeed,
J\theta=i\theta
since
\theta
is valued in vertical bundle and
\Phi(JM)=i\Phi\mu
by definition of
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
. In particular,
\mathcal{C}(P)
is a holomorphic submanifold of
\mathcal{J}(P)\times\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
and so inherits a complex structure
\mathrm{I}
. Explicitly,

\mathrm{I}_{(J,\Phi)}:\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)\longrightarrow\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)~:~(M,\theta)\longmapsto(JM,J\theta)=(JM,i\theta)\ .

Fix a structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}
to a maximal compact subgroup
\mathsf{K}
of
\mathsf{G}
.
Let
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
be a Higgs bundle and
A_{J}
be the associated Chern-Singer connection of
J
. The following lemma is immediate from the above discussion.

Lemma 3.4
.

In the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{A_{J}}
, a tangent vector
(M,\theta)\in\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
is given by

M=\begin{pmatrix}0&\beta\\
0&m\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \theta=\begin{pmatrix}0&\psi+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

where
\mu
is the Beltrami differential associated to
m\in\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
. In particular,
(M,\theta)
is uniquely determined by a tuple
(\mu,\beta,\psi),
where
\mu
is a Beltrami differential,
\beta\in\Omega_{b}^{0,1}(P,\mathfrak{g})
and
\psi\in\Omega_{b}^{1,0}(P,\mathfrak{g}).

3.2.
The holomorphic section
\Theta

As above, let
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}:\mathcal{C}(P)\to\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
be the holomorphic projection.
We now define a holomorphic section
\Theta
of the bundle
\Hom(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma),\ker(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\mathcal{J}}))
over
\mathcal{C}(P).

To start, consider the bundle map
\widehat{\Theta}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{C}(P)\to\mathrm{T}\mathcal{C}(P)
defined at
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)

\widehat{\Theta}_{(J,\Phi)}:\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)\longrightarrow\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)~:~(M,\theta)\longmapsto(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\circ M,0)\ .

Since
\Phi
takes values in the vertical bundle,
\widehat{\Theta}
takes values in the subbundle
\ker(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\mathcal{J}})
. Since
\Phi
is basic and has type
(1,0)
we have
\Phi\circ M=\Phi\mu
, where
\mu
is the Beltrami differential associated to
\mathrm{d}\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(M).

Hence,
\widehat{\Theta}
defines a bundle map

(3.3)

\Theta:\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\ker(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\mathcal{J}})~:~((J,\Phi),\mu)\longmapsto((J,\Phi),(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\circ\mu,0))\ .

Remark 3.5
.

As in Lemma
3.4
, fixing a structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
allows us to write tangent vectors
(M,\theta)
as
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
. In terms of this data, we have

\Theta(\mu)=(0,\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu,0).

Lemma 3.6
.

The bundle map
\Theta
is holomorphic.

Proof.

To prove the lemma, we will show that
\widehat{\Theta}
, seen as a smooth map from
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{C}(P)
to itself covering the identity, is holomorphic.The derivative of
\widehat{\Theta}
at
(J,\Phi,M,\theta)

\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Phi,M,\theta)}\widehat{\Theta}(X,\varphi,N,\vartheta)=(X,\varphi,\tfrac{1}{2i}\varphi\circ M+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\circ N,0).

The complex structure
\widehat{I}
on
\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi,M,\theta)}(\mathrm{T}\mathcal{C}(P))
is given by

\widehat{I}(X,\varphi,N,\vartheta)=(JX,i\varphi,JN,i\vartheta).

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Phi,M,\theta)}\widehat{\Theta}(\widehat{I}(X,\varphi,N,\vartheta))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Phi,M,\theta)}\widehat{\Theta}(JX,i\varphi,JN,i\vartheta)

\displaystyle=(JX,i\varphi,\tfrac{1}{2i}i\varphi\circ M+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\circ JN,0)

\displaystyle=(JX,i\varphi,i(\tfrac{1}{2i}\varphi\circ M+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\circ N),0),

where for the last equality we used
\Phi\circ JN=\Phi\circ(i\nu)
where
\nu
is the Beltrami differential associated to
d\pi(N).

Since
\varphi\circ M+\Phi\circ N
is a vertical vector, we have

(JX,i\varphi,i(\tfrac{1}{2i}\varphi\circ M+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\circ N),0)=\widehat{I}(X,\varphi,\tfrac{1}{2i}\varphi\circ M+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\circ N,0).

Thus,
\mathrm{d}\widehat{\Theta}
is complex linear as desired.
∎

3.3.
The closed 2-form
\omega_{0}
and the hermitian form
h_{0}

In this subsection we fix a structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
to
a maximal compact subgroup
\mathsf{K}<\mathsf{G}.
Recall that
-\tau(X)=X^{*}
is the
hermitian adjoint of
X
with respect to the hermitian metric

\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\cdot,-\tau(\cdot))
, where
\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}
is the Killing form.

3.3.1.
The Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form

For a principal
\mathsf{G}
-bundle
P,
the vector space
\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
has a complex symplectic form called the
Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form
. It is defined by

\omega^{\mathbb{C}}_{ABG}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}).

Since the space of connections
\mathcal{A}(P)
is affine over
\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
,
\omega_{ABG}^{\mathbb{C}}
defines a nondegenerate 2-form on
\mathcal{A}(P)
. Moreover,
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}_{ABG}
is closed since it is independent of the base point.

The structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
defines a real symplectic form on
\mathcal{A}(P)
which we denote by
\omega_{ABG}
.
Writing
\eta=X+iY
for
X,Y\in\Omega_{b}^{1}(P_{\mathsf{K}},\mathfrak{k})
, we have

\omega^{\mathbb{C}}_{ABG}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\omega^{\mathbb{C}}_{ABG}(X_{1}+iY_{1},X_{2}+iY_{2})=\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(X_{1}\wedge X_{2}-Y_{1}\wedge Y_{2})+i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(X_{1}\wedge Y_{2}+Y_{1}\wedge X_{2}).

The Killing form
\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}
is real on
\mathfrak{k}
,
\omega_{ABG}
is defined as the real part of
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}_{ABG}
:

(3.4)

\omega_{ABG}(X_{1}+iY_{1},X_{2}+iY_{2})=\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(X_{1}\wedge X_{2}-Y_{1}\wedge Y_{2}).

3.3.2.
The pullback of the Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form

Recall from (
2.4
) that the structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
defines the Chern-Singer isomorphism
\mathrm{S}:\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{A}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
, where we denote
\mathrm{S}(J)=A_{J}
. The defining property of
A_{J}
is
A_{J}\circ J=iA_{J}.
Consider the following smooth map

(3.5)

\mathrm{H}:\mathcal{J}(P)\times\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}(P)\ :\ (J,\Psi)\longmapsto A_{J}+\tfrac{1}{2i}(\Psi-\Psi^{*})

We start by computing the derivative of the Singer map.

Lemma 3.7
.

For
J\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, the differential of the Chern-Singer map
\mathrm{S}
at
J
is given by

\mathrm{d}_{J}\mathrm{S}(M)=\tfrac{1}{2i}(\beta+\beta^{*}),

where
M=\begin{pmatrix}0&\beta\\
0&m\end{pmatrix}
in the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{A_{J}}
associated to
A_{J}
.

Proof.

Consider a path
J_{t}\in\mathcal{J}(P)
with
J=J_{0}
and
\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{dt}}\right|_{t=0}J_{t}=M
. In the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{A_{J}}P
, we have

J_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}i&\alpha_{t}\\
0&j_{t}\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \mathrm{S}(J_{t})=A_{J_{t}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&\eta_{t}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

where
\eta_{t},\alpha_{t}\in\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
satisfy
\eta_{t}=-\eta_{t}^{*}
and
\alpha_{t}\circ J_{t}=i\alpha_{t}
and are both zero when
t=0
.

Denoting the derivatives of
\eta_{t}
at
t=0
by
\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta}
, we have
\mathrm{d}_{J}\mathrm{S}(M)=\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta}.

The equation
A_{J_{t}}\circ J_{t}=iA_{J_{t}}
is given by

\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&\eta_{t}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}i&\alpha_{t}\\
0&j_{t}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}i\mathrm{Id}&i\eta_{t}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Hence,
\alpha_{t}+\eta_{t}\circ j_{t}=i\eta_{t}
.
Differentiating at
0
and using
\eta_{0}=0
, we have

\beta+\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta}\circ j=i\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta},

where
\beta=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{dt}}\right|_{t=0}\alpha_{t}
.
Thus,
\beta=2i(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta})^{0,1}
, and so

\beta+\beta^{*}=2i(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta})^{0,1}-2i\left((\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta})^{0,1}\right)^{*}=2i(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta})^{0,1}+2i(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta})^{1,0}~,

where we used
\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta}=-\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\eta}^{*}
.
∎

The following lemma is now immediate.

Lemma 3.8
.

The derivative of the map
\mathrm{H}:\mathcal{J}(P)\times\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
at
(J,\Psi)
in the direction
(M,\theta)
is

\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Psi)}\mathrm{H}(M,\theta)=\tfrac{1}{2i}(\beta+\beta^{*})+\tfrac{1}{2i}(\theta-\theta^{*}),

where
M=\begin{pmatrix}0&\beta\\
0&m\end{pmatrix}
in the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{A_{J}}
associated to
A_{J}
.

Note that the decomposition
\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Psi)}\mathrm{H}(M,\theta)=X+iY
for
X,Y\in\Omega^{1}(P_{\mathsf{K}},\mathfrak{k})
is given by

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 36.16808pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-36.16808pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X=\tfrac{1}{2i}(\beta+\beta^{*})}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 60.16808pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 106.27922pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta^{*})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Using (
3.4
), the pullback of
\omega_{ABG}
by
\mathrm{H}
is thus given by

(3.6)

\textstyle{H^{*}\omega_{ABG}((M_{1},\theta_{1}),(M_{2},\theta_{2}))}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{-\dfrac{1}{4}\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\beta_{1}\wedge\beta_{2}^{*}+\beta_{1}^{*}\wedge\beta_{2})}
\textstyle{+\dfrac{1}{4}\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}^{*}+\theta_{1}^{*}\wedge\theta_{2})}
\textstyle{-\dfrac{1}{4}\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}+\theta_{1}^{*}\wedge\theta_{2}^{*})}

Lemma 3.9
.

The first two integrals in (
3.6
) are
\mathrm{I}
-invariant and the third integral is
\mathrm{I}
-anti-invariant. Moreover, all three integrals define closed
2-forms on
\mathcal{J}(P)\times\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g}).

Proof.

For
(J,\Psi)\in\mathcal{J}(P)\times\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
, recall the complex structure
\mathrm{I}
acts on a tangent vector
(M,\theta)=(\mu,\beta,\theta)
by
\mathrm{I}(\mu,\beta,\theta)=(i\mu,i\beta,i\theta)
. Hence, the first two integrals are
\mathrm{I}
-invariant, and the third is
\mathrm{I}
-anti-invariant.
The last two integrals are closed because they are independent of the
base point
(J,\Psi)
in
\mathcal{J}(P)\times\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
. The
first
integral is now closed because
\omega_{ABG}
is closed.
∎

3.3.3.
The closed 2-form
\omega_{0}
and hermitian form
h_{0}

We denote by
\mathrm{H}:\mathcal{C}(P)\to\mathcal{A}(P)
, the restriction of the map
\mathrm{H}
from (
3.5
) to the configuration space of Higgs bundles
\mathcal{C}(P)
.
By Lemma
3.9
, the restriction of the
\mathrm{I}
-invariant part of
\mathrm{H}^{*}\omega_{ABG}
to
\mathcal{C}(P)
is closed.

Definition 3.10
.

The closed 2-form
\omega_{0}
on
\mathcal{C}(P)
is defined to be the
\mathrm{I}
-invariant part of the pullback of the Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form by the map
\mathrm{H}
.
Denote the associated hermitian form by
h_{0}
:

h_{0}(v_{1},v_{2})=2(\omega_{0}(\mathrm{I}(v_{1}),v_{2})+i\omega_{0}(v_{1},v_{2})).

Lemma 3.11
.

Fix
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
and tangent vectors
v_{1},v_{2}
with
v_{a}=(\mu_{a},\beta_{a},\theta_{a})
and
\theta_{a}=\psi_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu_{a}
. Then

(1)

the form
\omega_{0}
is given by

\displaystyle\omega_{0}(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\beta_{1}\wedge\beta_{2}^{*}+\beta_{1}^{*}\wedge\beta_{2})+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}^{*}+\theta_{1}^{*}\wedge\theta_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\beta_{1}\wedge\beta_{2}^{*}+\beta_{1}^{*}\wedge\beta_{2})+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}^{*}+\psi_{1}^{*}\wedge\psi_{2})

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\frac{1}{16}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi\mu_{1}\wedge\Phi^{*}\overline{\mu}_{2}+\Phi^{*}\overline{\mu}_{1}\wedge\Phi\mu_{2})~.

(2)

The hermitian form
h_{0}
is equal to

h_{0}(v_{1},v_{2})=\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle+\langle\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\rangle-\tfrac{1}{4}\langle\Phi\mu_{1},\Phi\mu_{2}\rangle,

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the
L^{2}
-inner products from Lemma
2.3
.

Proof.

For
\omega_{0}
, the first equation follows from (
3.6
). The second follows from
\theta_{a}=\psi_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu_{a}
and
\theta_{a}^{*}=\psi_{a}^{*}-\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi^{*}\overline{\mu}_{a}
, and the fact that
\psi_{a}
and
\Phi\mu_{a}
has type
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, respectively.

For the second item, we compute
h_{0}(v_{1},v_{2})=2\omega_{0}(\mathrm{I}(v_{1}),v_{2})+i2\omega_{0}(v_{1},v_{2})
to be

h_{0}(v_{1},v_{2})=-i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\beta_{1}\wedge\beta_{2}^{*})+i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}^{*})+\frac{i}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi\mu_{1}\wedge\Phi^{*}\overline{\mu}_{2})

The result follows from
\beta
and
\Phi\mu
being
(0,1)
-forms,
\psi
being a
(1,0)
-form and Lemma
2.3
.
∎

3.4.
The family of forms
\omega_{s}
and
h_{s}

Unlike the Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form
\omega_{ABG}
, the closed 2-form

\omega_{0}
(equivalently the hermitian form
h_{0}
) is not everywhere
nondegenerate. Even when
h_{0}
is nondegenerate, it might not be positive
definite. In this section, we modify these forms by adding a multiple of
the Weil-Petersson form on
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
to obtain a positive definite form

h_{s_{0}}
on the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
. The scale
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
will depend on the point
(J,\Phi)
.

3.4.1.
Weil-Petersson form

For each complex structure
j\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
on
\Sigma,
let
\rho
denote the associated conformal Riemannian metric with constant curvature
-1
. This choice defines nondegenerate 2-form
\omega_{WP}
on
\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
called the Weil-Petersson form. For tangent vectors
m_{1},m_{2}\in T_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, it is defined by

\omega_{WP}(m_{1},m_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(m_{1}jm_{2})\nu_{\rho}=\frac{1}{2i}\int_{\Sigma}(\mu_{1}\overline{\mu}_{2}-\mu_{2}\overline{\mu}_{1})\,\nu_{\rho}~,

where
\nu_{\rho}
is the area form associated to
\rho
(see Appendix

A
for our conventions).
The associated hermitian form is given by

h_{WP}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=2\omega_{WP}(i\mu_{1},\mu_{2})+2i\omega_{WP}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\int_{\Sigma}\mu_{1}\overline{\mu}_{2}\,\nu_{\rho}~.

The Weil-Petersson form
\omega_{WP}
is
not
closed on
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
,
but it does descend to a closed form on the Teichmüller space of
\Sigma
,
see the proof of Corollary
4.15
below.

3.4.2.
The forms
\omega_{s}
and
h_{s}

Definition 3.12
.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, let
\omega_{s}
be the 2-form on
\mathcal{C}(P)
defined by

\omega_{s}=s\cdot\omega_{WP}+\omega_{0}.

The associated hermitian form will denoted by
h_{s}=s\cdot h_{WP}+h_{0}
.

Explicitly for tangent vectors
v_{1},v_{2}
with

v_{a}=(\mu_{a},\beta_{a},\psi_{a})
, the hermitian form
h_{s}
is given by

(3.7)

h_{s}(v_{1},v_{2})=s\cdot h_{WP}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})-\tfrac{1}{4}\langle\Phi\mu_{1},\Phi\mu_{2}\rangle+\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle+\langle\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\rangle\ .

Equivalently, we have

h_{s}(v_{1},v_{2})=\int_{\Sigma}(s-\tfrac{1}{4}|\Phi|^{2})\mu_{1}\overline{\mu}_{2}\,\nu_{\rho}-\frac{i}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\beta_{1}\wedge\beta_{2}^{*})+\frac{i}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}^{*}),

where
|\Phi|
is the pointwise norm of
\Phi
.
The following is immediate.

Lemma 3.13
.

For each
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
, there exists
s_{0}>0
such that
h_{s}
is
positive definite on
\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
for any
s>s_{0}
.

3.5.
Exponential maps

In our moduli space construction we will need a holomorphic “exponential map” from the tangent space of
\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
to
\mathcal{C}(P)
. This is more involved than the exponential map for the affine space of connections.

3.5.1.
Almost complex structures

Following
[
92
, §4]
, we describe explicit local
\mathrm{I}
-holomorphic charts on
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
centered at
j
.

Definition 3.14
.

For a complex structure
j\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, the exponential map at
j
is defined by

\exp_{j}:U_{j}\subset T_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)~:~m\longmapsto\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)\circ j\circ\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)^{-1},

where
U_{j}
is the subset of where
\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)
is invertible.

In terms of Beltrami differentials, one computes that
U_{j}\subset T_{j}\mathcal{J}(S)
is the complex subset

U_{j}=\left\{\mu\in\Omega^{0,1}(T^{1,0}_{j}\Sigma)~,~|\mu|^{2}<4\right\}.

The following lemma justifies calling the map in Definition
3.14

an “exponential map.”

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
j\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, the exponential map
\exp_{j}
satisfies the following:

(1)

\exp_{j}(0)=j
,

(2)

\mathrm{d}_{0}\exp_{j}=\mathrm{Id}
, and

(3)

\exp_{j}
is holomorphic.

Proof.

Item (1) is obvious. For item (2) and (3), fix
j\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
and set
k=\exp_{j}(m).

The differential of
\exp_{j}
at
m\in\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{m}\exp_{j}(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}j\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}j\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)^{-1}-\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)j\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)^{-1}\frac{j\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}}{2}\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{Id}-kj)\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)^{-1},

where we used that tangent vectors
\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}\in\mathrm{T}_{m}(\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
satisfies
j\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}+\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}j=0
.
Evaluating at
m=0
, we have
\mathrm{d}_{0}\exp_{j}=\mathrm{Id}
, proving item (2). Item (3) is equivalent to
D_{m}\exp_{j}\circ j=k\circ D_{m}\exp_{j}
. The result follows from direct computation

\mathrm{d}_{m}\exp_{j}\circ j=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{Id}-kj)j\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)^{-1}=\tfrac{1}{2}(j+k)\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)^{-1}=k\circ\mathrm{d}_{m}\exp_{j}.

∎

Analogous to Definition
3.14
, for
J\in\mathcal{J}(P)
define the exponential map

\exp_{J}:U_{J}\subset T_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)\longrightarrow\mathcal{J}(P)~:~M\longmapsto\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)J\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)^{-1}~,

where
U_{J}=\{M\in T_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)~|~\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)\text{ is invertible}\}
.
Parallel to Lemma
3.15
, we have that
\exp_{J}
is holomorphic and satisfies
\exp_{J}(0)=J
and
\mathrm{d}_{0}\exp_{J}=\mathrm{Id}.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
B\in\mathcal{A}(P)
be a connection compatible with
J\in\mathcal{J}(P)
, let
j=\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(J)
and write tangent vectors
M=(\beta,m)\in\mathrm{T}_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)
as in (
3.1
). Then, in the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}
, we have

(1)

U_{J}\cong\Omega^{0,1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})\times U_{j}
,

(2)

\exp_{J}(\beta,m)=\begin{pmatrix}i&\beta\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)^{-1}\\
0&\exp_{j}(m)\end{pmatrix}

Proof.

For Item (1), in the splitting
\mathrm{T}P=\mathrm{V}P\oplus\mathrm{H}_{B}
we have

\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&\tfrac{1}{2}i\beta\\
0&\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\end{pmatrix}.

Hence
\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)
is invertible if and only if
\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)
is invertible. Specifically,

\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&\tfrac{1}{2i}\beta\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)^{-1}\\
0&\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}jm\right)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Item (2) now follows by a direct computation using that
\beta
has type
(0,1)
with respect to
j
.
∎

3.5.2.
Higgs bundles

Before defining the exponential map for the configuration space
\mathcal{C}(P)
of Higgs bundles, we prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.17
.

Fix
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
and a tangent vector
M\in\mathrm{T}_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)
. Then, for
\eta\in\Omega^{(1,0)_{J}}(P,\mathrm{T}P),
we have

(\Phi+\eta)\left(\mathrm{Id}-\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)\circ\exp_{J}(M)=i\cdot(\Phi+\eta)\left(\mathrm{Id}-\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right).

Proof.

Using the definition of
\exp_{J}(M)
, we compute

\displaystyle(\Phi+\eta)\left(\mathrm{Id}-\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)\circ\exp_{J}(M)

\displaystyle=(\Phi+\eta)\left(\mathrm{Id}-\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)J\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\Phi+\eta)\left(\mathrm{Id}-\tfrac{1}{4}JMJM\right)J\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\Phi+\eta)J\left(\mathrm{Id}-\tfrac{1}{4}M^{2}\right)\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=i\cdot(\Phi+\eta)\left(\mathrm{Id}-\tfrac{1}{4}M^{2}\right)\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=i\cdot(\Phi+\eta)\left(\mathrm{Id}-\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)

where we used
J^{2}=-\mathrm{Id}
,
JM=-MJ
and
(\Phi+\eta)J=i(\Phi+\eta)
.
∎

With the above lemma, we define the exponential map for
\mathcal{C}(P)
as follows.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
, and for
(M,\theta)\in\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
, write
\theta=\psi+\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu
. The exponential map at
(J,\Phi)
is defined by

\exp_{(J,\Phi)}(M,\theta)=\left(\exp_{J}(M),(\Phi+\psi)\left(\mathrm{Id}-\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)\right),

where the domain is the set
V_{J}=\left\{(M,\theta)\in\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)~,~\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)\text{ is invertible}\right\}
.

As with
\mathcal{J}(P)
, the exponential map satisfies the following.

Lemma 3.19
.

For each
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
, the exponential map
\exp_{(J,\Phi)}
is holomorphic and satisfies

(1)

\exp_{(J,\Phi)}(0)=(J,\Phi)
, and

(2)

\mathrm{d}_{0}\exp_{(J,\Phi)}=\mathrm{Id}
,

Proof.

The first item is obvious. For
M\in\mathrm{T}_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)
, set
K=\exp_{J}(M)
. The differential of
\exp_{(J,\Phi)}
at
(M,\theta)\in\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
is

\mathrm{d}_{(M,\theta)}\exp_{(J,\Phi)}(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{M},\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\theta})=\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{Id}-KJ)\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{M}\left(\mathrm{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}JM\right)^{-1},\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\psi}(\mathrm{Id}-\tfrac{1}{2}JM)+(\Phi+\psi)(-\tfrac{1}{2}J\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{M})\right),

where
\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\theta}=\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\psi}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{M}
, and the first term is computed as in the proof of Lemma
3.15
.
The second item now follows by evaluating at
(M,\psi)=0
. Namely,

\mathrm{d}_{0}\exp_{(J,\Phi)}(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{M},\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\theta})=(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{M},\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\psi}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{M})=(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{M},\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\theta}),

where we used that
\Phi\circ J=i\Phi
.
The exponential map is holomorphic since the complex structure at
K
is given by postcomposing the first factor by
K
and multiplying the second factor by
i
. The first term is holomorphic since
\exp_{J}
is holomorphic.
∎

Fix a reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
of the structure group to a maximal
compact
\mathsf{K}<\mathsf{G}
.
Let
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
be a Higgs bundle and
A_{J}

the Chern-Singer connection associated to
J
.
The following lemma is immediate from the above discussion.

Lemma 3.20
.

In terms of the tangent data
(\mu,\beta,\psi),
the exponential map
\exp_{(J,\Phi)}:\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)\to\mathcal{C}(P)
is

\exp_{(J,\Phi)}(\mu,\beta,\psi)=\left(\exp_{J}(\mu,\beta),~\Phi+\psi+\tfrac{1}{2i}(\Phi+\psi)\mu\right).

4.
Construction of the joint moduli space

This section is devoted to the construction of the joint moduli space

\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
of stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles.
This moduli space is a complex orbifold equipped with a holomorphic submersion to
Teichmüller space
and a holomorphic action of the mapping class group of the surface
\Sigma
.
Furthermore,
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
admits an exhaustion by open sets
\mathcal{U}_{s}
equipped with mapping class group invariant Kähler forms
\omega_{s}
, see Theorem
4.6
.
We have included a fair number of details in this section which are likely well known to experts. We hope the exposition benefits those who are not familiar with the various subtleties.

4.1.
The automorphism group

An
automorphism
of
P
is a
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant diffeomorphism
\varphi:P\to P
. Such an automorphism preserves the fibers of
P
, and so covers a diffeomorphism of
\Sigma
. We will be interested in the subgroup
\Aut_{0}(P)<\Aut(P)
which covers the identity component
{\rm Diff}_{0}(\Sigma)
of
{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)
. In particular, the group
\Aut_{0}(P)
fits into the short exact sequence

(4.1)

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}(P)\longrightarrow\Aut_{0}(P)\longrightarrow{\rm Diff}_{0}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow 0~.

The subgroup
\mathcal{G}(P)
is called the
gauge group
of
P
. We note that
\Aut_{0}(P)
is not necessarily connected since it has the same components as the gauge group
\mathcal{G}(P).

The quotient
\Aut(P)/\Aut_{0}(P)
is isomorphic to the mapping class group of
\Sigma
.

On the Lie algebra level, the above exact sequence gives

0\longrightarrow\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))\longrightarrow\aut(P)\longrightarrow\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,\mathrm{T}\Sigma)\longrightarrow 0~.

The Lie algebra
\aut(P)
of
\Aut(P)
consists of
\mathsf{G}
-invariant vector fields on
P
.
The Lie algebra of
\mathcal{G}(P)
consists of
\mathsf{G}
-invariant vertical vector fields on
P
, which is identified with sections of the adjoint bundle
P(\mathfrak{g})
.

For a structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
the groups
\mathsf{G}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
and
\Aut_{0}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
are defined analogously.

Remark 4.1
.

The invariance has the following two consequences which will be used later on.

(1)

If
V\in\aut(P)
vanishes on a
\mathsf{K}
-subbundle
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
, it is identically zero on
P
.

(2)

If
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, then
[X^{\sharp},V]=0
for all
V\in\aut(P)
.

The automorphism group
\Aut(P)
acts on the configurations space
\mathcal{C}(P)
from Definition
3.2
and the projection map
\pi:\mathcal{C}(P)\to\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
is equivariant with respect to the actions of
\Aut_{0}(P)
and
{\rm Diff}_{0}(\Sigma)
.

4.2.
The holomorphicity condition and the Hitchin equations

Recall from Definition
3.2
that the configuration space of Higgs bundles
\mathcal{C}(P)
is defined by

\mathcal{C}(P)=\{(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{J}(P)\times\Omega^{1}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})~|~\Phi\circ J=i\Phi\}~.

A pair
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
is called a
Higgs bundle
if
\Phi
is holomorphic with respect to
J.
The holomorphicity condition will be encoded as the zero level set of the map

(4.2)

F:\mathcal{C}(P)\longrightarrow\Omega^{2}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})~:~(J,\Phi)\longmapsto\bar{\partial}_{J}\Phi\ .

Recall that tangent vectors in
\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
are written
(\mu,\beta,\theta)
where
\theta=\psi+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu
, and
\psi
has type
(1,0)
. Recall also that the complex structure
\mathrm{I}
on
\mathcal{C}(P)
acts on
(\mu,\beta,\theta)
by multiplication by
i.

Lemma 4.2
.

The map
F
is holomorphic.

Proof.

Fix
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
and a reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
. By Lemma
2.6
,
F
is given by

F(J,\Phi)=\mathrm{d}\Phi+[A_{J},\Phi]=\left(\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\Phi\right)^{1,1}~,

where
A_{J}
is the Chern-Singer connection of
J
.
Using Lemma
3.7
we get

(4.3)

\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Phi)}F(\mu,\beta,\theta)=\mathrm{d}\theta+\tfrac{1}{2i}[\beta+\beta^{*},\Phi]+[A_{J},\theta]=\overline{\partial}_{J}\psi+\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\Phi\mu\right)^{1,1}+\tfrac{1}{2i}[\beta,\Phi]~,

where for the second equality we used the fact that this 2-form is basic, so its
(2,0)
and
(0,2)
parts both vanish. Hence,
\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Phi)}F(i\mu,i\beta,i\theta)=i\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Phi)}F(\mu,\beta,\theta)
and we conclude that
F
is holomorphic.
∎

To construct a moduli space of Higgs bundles on a fixed Riemann surface, it is necessary to restrict to the set of so-called
polystable

\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles. This notion, as well as the notions of (semi-)stability come from Geometric Invariant theory and are relevant for constructing the moduli space as an algebraic variety.
We will not need any of the technical definitions of stability for this
paper. Instead, we will use the equivalence between stability and
irreducible solutions
to the so called Hitchin equations. Before explaining this, we mention a
few important features.

Fixing, a Riemann surface
X=(\Sigma,j)
and recall there is a holomorphic projection
\pi:\mathcal{C}(P)\to\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
. The set

\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})=\{(J,\Phi)\in F^{-1}(0)~|~\pi(J,\Phi)=j\text{ and $(J,\Phi)$
is stable}\}/\mathcal{G}(P)

has the structure of a normal quasi-projective variety (with only orbifold
singularities) called the moduli space stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles on
X
.
The smooth locus of
{\bf M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})

consists of isomorphism classes of stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles whose
automorphism group is the center of
\mathsf{G}
. We will refer to such Higgs
bundles as
regularly stable
.

We will make use of the following property of stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles, see
[
12
]
. which we will use are the following.

Proposition 4.3
.

The automorphism group of a stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle is finite.
In particular, at stable points,

F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathcal{C}(P)
is locally a holomorphic submanifold.

We now describe the relation between stability and solutions to the so called Hitchin equation. This requires fixing a structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
to the maximal compact subgroup
\mathsf{K}<\mathsf{G}.
Consider the map
\mathcal{C}(P)\to\Omega^{2}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})
defined by

(J,\Phi)\mapsto F_{A_{J}}+\tfrac{1}{4}[\Phi,\Phi^{*}]~,

where
F_{A_{J}}
is the curvature of the Chern-Singer connection and
\Phi^{*}
is the adjoint of
\Phi.

The equation

(4.4)

F_{A_{J}}+\tfrac{1}{4}[\Phi,\Phi^{*}]=0

is called the
Hitchin equation
, and the set of
(J,\Phi)\in F^{-1}(0)
solving equation (
4.4
) is called the space of
solutions to the Hitchin equation
.

Recall from (
3.5
) that the map
\mathrm{H}:\mathcal{C}(P)\to\mathcal{A}(P)
sends

(J,\Phi)
to the
\mathsf{G}
-connection
D_{(J,\Phi)}:=A_{J}+\frac{1}{2i}(\Phi-\Phi^{*})
. For
(J,\Phi)
a solution to the Hitchin, the connection
D_{(J,\Phi)}
is flat. Indeed,

F_{D_{(J,\Phi)}}=F_{A_{J}}+\tfrac{1}{4}[\Phi,\Phi^{*}]+\tfrac{1}{2i}\bar{\partial}_{J}\Phi-\tfrac{1}{2i}\partial_{J}\Phi^{*}.

Remark 4.4
.

The factor of
\tfrac{1}{4}
appears naturally since the configuration space is defined in terms of almost complex complex structures instead of
\bar{\partial}
-operators, see Lemma
3.7
.

Since the Hitchin equation requires fixing a structure group reduction, the space of
solutions is preserved by the
\mathsf{K}
-gauge group
\mathcal{G}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
but not the

\mathsf{G}
-gauge group. The following theorem of Hitchin for
\mathsf{SL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, and
Simpson in general, relates solutions to the Hitchin equation and stability.

Theorem 4.5

(
[
47
,
81
]
)
.

A Higgs bundle
(J,\Phi)\in F^{-1}(0)
is polystable if and only if there exists a gauge transformation
g\in\mathcal{G}(P)
such that
g\cdot(J,\Phi)
solves the Hitchin equations (
4.4
). Moreover,
g
is unique up to the actions of the
\mathcal{G}(P)
-stabilizer of
(J,\Phi)
and the compact gauge group
\mathcal{G}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
. In particular, for
(J,\Phi)
stable,
g
is unique up to the action of
\mathcal{G}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
.

As a consequence, for each structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
to the maximal compact
\mathsf{K}<\mathsf{G}
, the moduli space of stable Higgs bundles on a fixed Riemann surface can be equivalently described as

\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})=\left\{(J,\Phi)\in F^{-1}(0)^{st}\mid(J,\Phi)\text{
satisfies \eqref{eq Hitchin eq} with }\pi(J,\Phi)=j\right\}/\mathcal{G}(P_{\mathsf{K}}).

4.3.
The joint moduli space

Fix a structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
. Consider the map

F
from (
4.2
), and denote the set of stable (resp. regularly stable) Higgs bundles by
F^{-1}(0)^{st}\subset\mathcal{C}(P)

(resp.
F^{-1}(0)^{rs}\subset\mathcal{C}(P)
).
The joint moduli space of stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles is the space

\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})=F^{-1}(0)^{st}/\Aut_{0}(P)=\{(J,\Phi)\in F^{-1}(0)^{st}\mid(J,\Phi)\text{ satisfies \eqref{eq Hitchin eq}}\}/\Aut_{0}(P_{\mathsf{K}}).

There is a natural projection to
\pi:\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
to the Teichmüller space of
\Sigma.
Moreover, the mapping class group
\mathsf{Mod}(\Sigma)
, identified with
\Aut(P)/\Aut_{0}(P)
, acts naturally on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
covering its standard action on
\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
.
Recall from §
3.4.2
that
\mathcal{C}(P)
has a 1-parameter family of closed 2-forms
\{\omega_{s}\}_{s\in\mathbb{R}}
and an associated family of hermitian forms
\{h_{s}\}_{s\in\mathbb{R}}
. Finally, recall the holomorphic bundle map
\Theta
from §
3.2
.

Theorem 4.6
.

The joint moduli space
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
of stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles on
\Sigma
is an orbifold equipped with an integrable complex structure
\mathrm{I}
, a holomorphic submersion
\pi:\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
to the Teichmüller space of
\Sigma
and a holomorphic bundle map

\Theta:\pi^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\ker(\mathrm{d}\pi).

In addition,
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
is equipped with a
1
-parameter family

\{\omega_{s}\}_{s\in\mathbb{R}}
of closed
2
-forms which are compatible with
\mathrm{I}
, and the associated hermitian forms
h_{s}
satisfy the following:

(1)

The open sets

\mathcal{U}_{s}:=\{p\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})~|~(h_{s})_{p}>0\}

define an increasing exhaustion of
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
as
s
goes to
+\infty
.

(2)

The restriction of
h_{s}
to
\pi^{-1}(j)
is independent of
s
,
positive and
\pi^{-1}(j)
is isomorphic as a Kähler manifold to
the moduli space of stable Higgs bundles on the fixed Riemann
surface
X=(\Sigma,j)
.

Moreover, all of these structures are invariant under the action of
\mathsf{Mod}(\Sigma)
.

Remark 4.7
.

We note also that
(2,0)
-part of the pullback of the
Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form defines a closed holomorphic 2-form on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
.

The rest of this section is devoted to setting up and proving the
Theorem
4.6
.
The standard approach to prove such a theorem is to construct a local slice for the action.
First, following Atiyah-Bott
[
7
, p. 577]
,
let us denote
\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P):=\Aut_{0}(P)/Z(\mathsf{G})
, where the inclusion

Z(\mathsf{G})\hookrightarrow\Aut_{0}(P)
is by constant gauge transformations in
the center
Z(\mathsf{G})
. Then
\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)
still acts on
\mathcal{C}(P)
.
Now, given a point
p
in
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
, and a lift
x\in F^{-1}(0)^{st}

which solves the Hitchin equations (
4.4
), we seek a holomorphic submanifold
S_{x}
of
F^{-1}(0)^{st}
through
x
such that the orbit map

\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)\times S_{x}\longrightarrow F^{-1}(0)^{st}

is a diffeomorphism onto an open neighborhood of
x
in
F^{-1}(0)^{st}

(or more generally a finite ramified cover).
If the slice is natural, in the sense that
S_{y}=g\cdot S_{x}
for
g\in\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
and
y=g\cdot x
, then the slices define a holomorphic (orbifold) atlas on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
.

By Lemma
3.13
, for sufficiently large
s
the hermitian form
h_{s}
is positive at
x
.
We use this metric to complete all relevant Fréchet spaces to Hilbert manifolds using the Sobolev topology.
The slice is constructed as follows: the deformation complex arising from the infinitesimal action of
\Aut_{0}(P)
on
\mathcal{C}(P)
and the variation of the holomorphicity equation
F
turns out to be elliptic, and so has a finite dimensional space of harmonics.
Applying the exponential map from Definition
3.18
to the
space of harmonics, and an implicit function theorem to project back to

F^{-1}(0)^{st}
, one obtains a complex submanifold
\mathcal{S}_{x}
of

F^{-1}(0)^{st}
. When
x
is regularly stable, this is shown to be a local slice using properness and
freeness of the action of
\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)
. For
x
stable but not
regularly stable, the stabilizer of
x
in
\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)
is finite and preserves
\mathcal{S}_{x}
, yielding an orbifold structure on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
.

Surprisingly, the harmonics at
x
, defined with respect to the metric
h_{s}
for
s
sufficiently large, are independent of the parameter
s
.
Using the explicit form of the harmonics, we show that
\mathrm{d}\omega_{s}

vanishes on the tangent spaces to the slices for all
s
. For sufficiently
large
s
, this implies that the real part of
h_{s}
defines
a Kähler metric in a neighborhood of
p\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
.

4.4.
Sobolev completion

In order to work with Hilbert manifolds, it is classical to complete the different spaces. Fixing a background metric on
\Sigma
and structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset\ P
define the Sobolev
W^{k,2}
-norm on spaces of tensors valued in associated bundles (where the
W^{k,2}
-norm is defined using the
L^{2}
-norm of the first
k
derivatives). This norm can be used to complete
\mathcal{C}(P)
into a Hilbert manifold
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
. Compactness of
\Sigma
implies that the
W^{k,2}
-topology is independent of the choices.

Similarly,
\Aut_{0}(P)
has a Sobolev completion
\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}
and the
different maps (exponential map and holomorphicity) extend smoothly to those completions and their extension will be denoted with an exponent
k
. We will refer to the subspaces
\mathcal{C}(P)
and
\Aut_{0}(P)
of the respective completions as the smooth tensors (or symmetries). We will also assume
k
large enough to ensure our tensors to be
C^{2}
and that
W^{k,2}
is closed under taking products.

Let us highlight two classical issues coming with this completion:

(1)

The Hilbert topology and the Fréchet topology on
\mathcal{C}(P)
are different.

(2)

Even if the action of
\Aut_{0}(P)
on
\mathcal{C}(P)
is smooth, that
of
\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}
on
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
is not.

The first issue will be fixed using elliptic regularity: the equation
F(x)=0
is elliptic and thus the Sobolev and smooth topology will coincide on
F^{-1}(0)
.
For the second issue, even is the action of
\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}
on
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
is not smooth, given
x\in\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
which is a smooth tensor, the orbit map

\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}~:~g\longmapsto gx

is smooth. We now gather some properties of the action of
\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}
on
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

The action of
\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}
on
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
is proper, and acts with
finite stabilizer on the space of stable Higgs bundles. The stabilizer
is
Z(\mathsf{G})
for regularly stable Higgs bundles.
Moreover, given a smooth
x\in\mathcal{C}(P)
and an element
g\in\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}
, if
gx\in\mathcal{C}(P)
, then
g\in Aut_{0}(P)
.

Proof.

The proof will follow from the same statement for the action of
{\rm Diff}^{k+1}_{0}(\Sigma)
on
\mathcal{J}^{k}(\Sigma)
proved by Tromba
[
92
]
as well as for the action of
\mathcal{G}^{k+1}(P)
on
1
-forms proved by Freed-Uhlenbeck
[
41
]
.

Indeed, for properness, let
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences in
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
and
\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}
respectively such that
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(g_{n}x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converge. Since the projection
\pi:\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}\to\mathcal{J}^{k}(\Sigma)
is equivariant under the map
p:\Aut^{k+1}(P)\to{\rm Diff}^{k+1}(\Sigma)
, we get that
(\pi(x_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(p(g_{n})\pi(x_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
both converge in
\mathcal{J}^{k+1}(\Sigma)
. By the properness of the action of
{\rm Diff}_{0}^{k+1}(\Sigma)
on
\mathcal{J}^{k}(\Sigma)
(see
[
92
, Theorem 2.3.1]
), up to extracting, we have that
(p(g_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in
{\rm Diff}_{0}^{k+1}(\Sigma)
.

For any
n
, let
h_{n}
be a lift of
p(g_{n})^{-1}
to
\Aut_{0}^{k+1}(P)
such that
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges. The sequence
(h_{n}x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(h_{n}g_{n}x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are then converging sequences in a fixed fiber of
\pi
, and one can apply Freed-Uhlenbeck properness
[
41
, Proposition A.5]
: up to extracting again, the sequence
(h_{n}g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in
\mathcal{G}^{k+1}(P)
. Since
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
already converges,
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in
\Aut_{0}^{k+1}(P)
. This proves properness.

To show that if
x
and
gx
are both in
\mathcal{C}(P)
then
g
must be in

\Aut_{0}(P)
we follow the same trick: projecting to
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
and
applying
[
92
, Remark 2.4.3]
we get that
p(g)
is in

{\rm Diff}_{0}(\Sigma)
. Taking a lift
h
of
p(g)
in
\Aut_{0}(P)
, we get
that
hg
is a gauge transformation
in
\mathcal{G}^{k+1}(P)
mapping a smooth element
x\in\mathcal{C}(P)
to a smooth
element
hgx\in\mathcal{C}(P)
. Hence,
hg\in\mathcal{G}(P)
by
[
41
, Proposition
A.5]
, and so
g\in\Aut_{0}(P)
.

Finally, freeness of the action of
\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)^{k+1}
on regularly stable
Higgs bundles follow from freeness of the action of

{\rm Diff}^{k+1}_{0}(\Sigma)
on
\mathcal{J}^{k}(\Sigma)
(see
[
92
, Theorem
2.2.1]
) and freeness of the action of
the gauge group
\mathcal{G}(P)/Z(\mathsf{G})
on regularly stable Higgs bundles. The finite stabilizer property is proved analogously.
∎

4.5.
Deformation complex

Fix a stable Higgs bundle
(J,\Phi)\in F^{-1}(0)^{st}
. By stability, up to acting by an element in
\Aut_{0}(P)
, we can furthermore assume that
(J,\Phi)
satisfies the Hitchin equations (
4.4
). The deformation complex in our situation is given by

(4.5)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 22.99652pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.99652pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 306.58505pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 306.58505pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where the map
\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{0}
is the derivative at the identity of the action
of
\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}
on
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
, i.e., taking the Lie derivative of
(J,\Phi)
, and
\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}
is the derivative of
F^{k}
at
0.

Consider the
\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}
-equivariant projection
\pi^{k}:\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}\to\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)^{k}
. For
j=\pi(J,\Phi)
, we get a
subcomplex
(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet},\delta_{\mathrm{A}}^{\bullet})
isomorphic to the deformation complex for Higgs bundles on the fixed Riemann surface
(\Sigma,j)
and a quotient deformation complex isomorphic to the deformation complex for
j\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma).
We thus obtain an exact sequence of complexes of Hilbert spaces

0^{\bullet}\longrightarrow(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet},\delta_{\mathrm{A}}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet},\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow(\mathrm{C}^{\bullet},\delta_{\mathrm{C}}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow 0^{\bullet}~.

Denote
(\pi^{k})^{-1}(j)
by
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}_{j}
, the complexes
(A^{\bullet},\delta_{\mathrm{A}}^{\bullet})
and
(\mathrm{C}^{\bullet},\delta_{C}^{\bullet})
are given by

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 23.3611pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.3611pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet},\delta_{\mathrm{A}}^{\bullet}):}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 47.3611pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.3611pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 82.3611pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{0}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 131.38094pt\raise 6.62001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.62001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{\mathrm{A}}^{0}\hskip 8.19447pt}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 151.24315pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 151.24315pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 213.53801pt\raise 6.62001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.62001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{\mathrm{A}}^{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 235.14049pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 235.14049pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 310.70311pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 310.70311pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-23.12498pt\raise-8.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\mathrm{C}^{\bullet},\delta_{\mathrm{C}}^{\bullet}):}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 47.3611pt\raise-8.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.37674pt\raise-8.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 82.37674pt\raise-8.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{0}(\mathrm{T}\Sigma)^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 135.53374pt\raise-2.37001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.62001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{\mathrm{C}}^{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 159.94438pt\raise-8.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 159.94438pt\raise-8.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 255.4218pt\raise-8.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 255.4218pt\raise-8.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 313.20311pt\raise-8.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The maps
\delta_{\mathrm{A}}^{0}
and
\delta_{\mathrm{C}}^{0}
are the derivative at the identity of the actions of the gauge group
\mathcal{G}(P)
and the diffeomorphism group, respectively, and the map
\delta_{A}^{1}
is the differential of the holomorphicity condition.

We now give a more explicit description of the above maps, which will serve
to simplify the computations of the harmonics in the next section. Recall that we are assuming
(J,\Phi)\in F^{-1}(0)^{st}
solves the Hitchin equations.
Following Simpson, we introduce the following operators acting on

P(\mathfrak{g})
-valued forms on
\Sigma
,

(4.6)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 34.80083pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-34.80083pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime\prime}=\bar{\partial}_{A_{J}}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 58.80083pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 104.91197pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime}=\partial_{A_{J}}+(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi)^{\ast}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The holomorphicity condition implies
(D^{\prime\prime})^{2}=0
and
(D^{\prime})^{2}=0
, and the assumption that
(J,\Phi)
solves the Hitchin equations (
4.4
) for the reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}
implies
D^{\prime\prime}D^{\prime}=-D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}
.

The maps
\delta_{\mathrm{A}}^{0}
and
\delta_{\mathrm{A}}^{1}
are defined by applying the operator
D^{\prime\prime}
to the appropriate spaces.
In terms of the operators
D^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}
, the map
\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}
is described as follows.

Lemma 4.9
.

For
(\mu,\beta,\theta)\in\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
with
\theta=\psi+\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu
, we have

\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}(\mu,\beta,\theta)=D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)+D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right).

Proof.

The map
\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}
is the differential of the map
F
at
0
. By (
4.3
), we have

\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}(\mu,\beta,\theta)=d\theta+\tfrac{1}{2i}[\beta+\beta^{*},\Phi]+[A_{J},\theta]=\bar{\partial}_{J}\psi+\left[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi,\beta\right]+\partial_{A_{J}}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right),

where we used that the
(0,2)
and
(2,0)
parts vanish. The first two terms are
D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)
and third term is
D^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)
.
∎

We now give an explicit description of
\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{0}.
For
V\in\aut(P)
,
we will denote the contraction of
V
with the Chern-Singer connection by
\eta_{V}=A_{J}(V)
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose
(J,\Phi)
is a stable Higgs bundle satisfying (
4.4
) such that
\pi(J,\Phi)=j
. Let
V\in\aut(P)^{k}
and denote the projected vector field by
v\in\Omega^{0}(\mathrm{T}^{(1,0)}_{j}\Sigma)^{k}
.
Writing
\delta_{B}^{0}(V)=(\mu,\beta,\theta)
, we have

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 24.11171pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.11171pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mu=2i\bar{\partial}_{j}v}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 48.11171pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 93.95607pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\beta,\theta)=2iD^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{V})+D^{\prime}(\Phi(V))+D^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(V))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Proof.

We first compute the action of
\aut(P)^{k}
on the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)^{k}
.

Denote this by

\aut(P)^{k}\longrightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathcal{J}(P)^{k}~:~V\mapsto V^{\sharp}_{J}=L_{V}\circ J-J\circ L_{V}\in\Omega^{0}_{b}(\End(\mathrm{T}P))\ ,

where
L_{V}
denotes the Lie derivative.

For general vector field
W\in\Omega^{0}(\mathrm{T}P)
, denote the vertical and
horizontal parts with respect to the Chern-Singer connection by
W_{v}
and
W_{h}
.
By Remark
4.1
,
V^{\sharp}_{J}(W)=V^{\sharp}_{J}(W_{h})
.
Hence,

\displaystyle V^{\sharp}_{J}(W)

\displaystyle=[V,jW_{h}]-J([V,W_{h}])

\displaystyle=[\eta_{V},jW_{h}]-J([\eta_{V},W_{h}])+[V_{h},jW_{h}]-J([V_{h},W_{h}])

The decomposition of the image of
V^{\sharp}_{J}
into its vertical and horizontal parts corresponds to the decomposition of the tangent vector
M=(m,\beta)
from (
3.1
), where
\mu
is the
(0,1)
-part of
m
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle\beta(W)

\displaystyle=[\eta_{V},jW_{h}]_{v}-i[\eta_{V},W_{h}]_{v}+[V_{h},jW_{h}]_{v}-i[V_{h},W_{h}]_{v}

\displaystyle=2i([\eta_{V},W_{h}^{(0,1)_{J}}]_{v}+[V_{h},W_{h}^{(0,1)_{J}}]_{v})

Using
F_{A_{J}}(V,W)=-[V_{h},W_{h}]_{v}
has type
(1,1)
and the Hitchin equations, we have

\beta=2i\left(\bar{\partial}_{J}(\eta_{V})+\iota_{V^{(1,0)_{J}}}F_{A_{J}}\right)=2i\left(\bar{\partial}_{J}(\eta_{V})-\tfrac{1}{4}\left[\Phi(V),\Phi^{*}\right]\right)=2i\bar{\partial}_{J}(\eta_{V})+\left[\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*},\Phi(V)\right].

The horizontal piece depends only on the projected vector fields
v^{\prime},w^{\prime}
of
V,W
. Thus,

m(w^{\prime})=[v^{\prime},jw^{\prime}]-j([v^{\prime},w^{\prime}]).

Denoting the
(1,0)
-part of
v^{\prime}
by
v
, the
(0,1)
-part of
m
is given by

\mu=2i\bar{\partial}_{j}v
.
Finally we compute the Higgs field term. For a vector field
W\in\Omega^{0}(\mathrm{T}P)
we have

L_{V}\Phi(W)=d\Phi(V,W)+W\Phi(V).

Since
\Phi
is basic, the (2,0) part of
d\Phi+[A_{J},\Phi]
vanishes. By the holomorphicity condition, we have

\displaystyle L_{V}\Phi(W)

\displaystyle=-[A_{J},\Phi](V,W)+W\Phi(V)

\displaystyle=[\Phi(W),\eta_{V}]+[A_{J}(W),\Phi(V)]+W\Phi(V)

\displaystyle=\left([\Phi,\eta_{V}]+d_{A_{J}}\Phi(V)\right)(W)

Adding the expressions for
\beta
and
L_{V}\Phi
and using
[\Phi,\Phi(V)]=0
gives the desired result.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

The
(0,1)
-part of
L_{V}\Phi
is
D^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(V))
. This is indeed
\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu
since, using the notation above,

D^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(V))=\bar{\partial}_{J}(\Phi(V))=\bar{\partial}_{J}(\Phi(v))=\Phi(\bar{\partial}_{j}v)=\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi(\mu),

where we used the expression for
v
from Lemma
4.10
, and that
\Phi
is basic and holomorphic.

Lemma 4.12
.

The sequence
(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet},\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{\bullet})
is a complex, i.e.,
\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}\circ\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{0}=0.

Proof.

Let
V\in\aut(P)
. Then, by the above remark,

\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}(\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{0}(V))=D^{\prime\prime}(2iD^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{V})+D^{\prime}(\Phi(V))+D^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(V)))+D^{\prime}(D^{\prime\prime}\Phi(V)).

The result follows from the fact that
(D^{\prime\prime})^{2}=0
and
D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(V))=-D^{\prime\prime}D^{\prime}(\Phi(V)).

∎

Lemma 4.13
.

For
(J,\Phi)
stable, the sequence

0\longrightarrow H^{1}(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow H^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow H^{1}(\mathrm{C}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow 0

is exact and all other cohomology groups vanish.

Proof.

Since
{\rm Diff}_{0}(\Sigma)
acts freely on
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, the cohomology group

H^{0}(\mathrm{C}^{\bullet})
is vanishes. The assumption that
(J,\Phi)
is
stable and
\mathsf{G}
is semisimple implies that
H^{0}(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet})=0
and,
by Serre duality,

H^{2}(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet})=0
, see Proposition
4.3
.
The statement now follows from the associated long exact sequence in cohomology.
∎

4.6.
Harmonic and semiharmonic representatives

Recall that
(J,\Phi)
is a fixed stable Higgs bundle which solves the Hitchin equation.
Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
be large enough such that the hermitian form
h_{s}
is positive on
\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
, and use it to define the different adjoints in the above diagram. Recall that, at
(J,\Phi)
,
h_{s}
involves the hyperbolic metric on
\Sigma
which uniformizes the induced Riemann surface.
Denote the adjoints of
\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{0}
and
\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}
with respect to

h_{s}
by
(\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{0})^{*}
and
(\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1})^{*}
, respectively. The
adjoints of the operators
D^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}
from (
4.6
)
satisfy the Kähler identities (see Appendix
A
):

(4.7)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 43.56422pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-43.56422pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(D^{\prime\prime})^{\ast}=-i[\Lambda,D^{\prime}]\quad}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 67.56422pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 113.67537pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\quad(D^{\prime})^{\ast}=i[\Lambda,D^{\prime\prime}]}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
\Lambda

denotes the contraction with the area form of the hyperbolic metric on
X
.

Standard Hodge theory yields the following orthogonal decomposition

\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}=\mathrm{Im}(\delta^{0}_{\mathrm{B}})\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})\oplus\mathrm{Im}((\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1})^{*})~,

where
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})=\ker(\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1})\cap\ker((\delta^{0}_{\mathrm{B}})^{*})
is
the
space of harmonics
which is
naturally identified with
H^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})
.
The harmonics are given by the following proposition.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
(J,\Phi)
be a stable Higgs bundle satisfying (
4.4
), and
s\in\mathbb{R}
be large enough so that the hermitian form

h_{s}
is positive definite at
(J,\Phi)
. A tangent vector

(\mu,\beta,\theta)\in\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
with
\theta=\psi+\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu
is harmonic
with respect to
h_{s}
(that is, it is in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})
)
if and only if it satisfies

(1)

D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)+D^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)=0
;

(2)

D^{\prime}(\beta,\psi)=0
;

(3)

\bar{\partial}^{\ast}\mu=0
.

In particular, the space of harmonics is independent of the parameter
s
and consists of smooth tensors, i.e., vectors tangent to
\mathcal{C}(P)
in the Sobolev completion.

Proof.

Item (1) is the property of being in the kernel of
\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}
.
For (2), we compute the terms in the expression (
3.7
).
Let
(\mu,\beta,\psi)\in\ker(\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1})
. As above, let
\eta_{V}=A_{J}(V)
for any
V\in\aut(P)
and
(\mu_{1},\beta_{1},\theta_{1})=\delta_{B}^{0}(V)
.
From Lemma
4.10
, Remark
4.11
and the Kähler identities
(
4.7
) we have

\displaystyle\langle(\beta_{1},\psi_{1}),(\beta,\psi)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 2i\,D^{\prime\prime}\eta_{V},(\beta,\psi)\rangle+\langle D^{\prime}(\Phi(V)),(\beta,\psi)\rangle

\displaystyle=-2i\langle\eta_{V},i\Lambda D^{\prime}(\beta,\psi)\rangle+\langle\Phi(V),i\Lambda D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\eta_{V},2\Lambda D^{\prime}(\beta,\psi)\rangle-\langle\Phi(V),i\Lambda D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\rangle~,

where we have the definition of
\ker(\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1})
.
On the other hand

-\tfrac{1}{4}\langle\Phi\mu_{1},\Phi\mu\rangle~=~-\tfrac{i}{2}\langle D^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(V)),\Phi\mu\rangle~=~-\langle D^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(V)),\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\rangle~=~+\langle\Phi(V),i\Lambda D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\rangle.

It follows that

\langle\delta_{B}^{0}(V),(\mu,\beta,\theta)\rangle_{h_{s}}=-\langle\eta_{V},2\Lambda D^{\prime}(\beta,\psi)\rangle+2is\langle v,\bar{\partial}^{\ast}\mu\rangle_{WP}\ .

Since
V\in\aut(P)
was arbitrary, the result follows.
∎

We have the following corollary.

Corollary 4.15
.

For any
s
the form
\mathrm{d}\omega_{s}
vanishes on
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})
.

Proof.

Recall from §
3.4.2
that
\omega_{s}=s\cdot\omega_{WP}+\omega_{0}
, where
\omega_{0}
is the closed 2-form from
Definition
3.10
and
\omega_{WP}
is the Weil-Peterson form
on
