\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
. The result follows from the fact that the Weil-Petersson
symplectic form is closed on the space of harmonic Beltrami
differentials, see
[
1
,
96
]
.
∎

The hermitian form
h_{0}
from Definition
3.10
does not make use of a conformal metric on
\Sigma
, and so we have no notion of harmonic representatives for Beltrami
differentials. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 4.16
.

Let
(J,\Phi)
be a Higgs bundle satisfying (
4.4
). We say a vector

(\mu,\beta,\psi)\in\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
is
semiharmonic

if it satisfies

•

D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)+D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)=0
, (holomorphicity condition);

•

D^{\prime}(\beta,\psi)=0
, (gauge condition).

In other words, we impose no condition on the Beltrami differential (item
(3) in Proposition
4.14
).

There is an infinite dimensional
space of
semiharmonic representatives for a given cohomology
class in
H^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})
. The following result characterizes these.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
(J,\Phi)
be a Higgs bundle which solves the Hitchin equations,
V\in\aut(P)
and set
\eta_{V}=A_{J}(V)
. If
(\mu,\beta,\theta)
and

(\mu_{1},\beta_{1},\theta_{1})=(\mu,\beta,\theta)+\delta_{B}^{0}(V)

are both semiharmonic, then
V
is horizontal with respect to the
Chern-Singer connection
A_{J}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
4.10
, we have
\mu_{1}=\mu+2i\bar{\partial}v
, and

(\beta_{1},\psi_{1})=(\beta,\psi)+2iD^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{V})+D^{\prime}(\Phi(V))\ .

Applying the gauge condition to both sides, we have
D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{V})=0
,
and therefore
\eta_{V}\in H^{0}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})
.
But since stable implies simple,

\eta_{V}
must take values in the center. Since
\mathsf{G}
is semisimple,

\eta_{V}=0
, and therefore
V
is horizontal.
∎

An important consequence of the above is the following.

Proposition 4.18
.

The difference of any two semiharmonic representatives of a given cohomology class

[(\mu,\beta,\theta)]

lies in the kernel of the hermitian form
h_{0}
.
In particular,
h_{0}
is a well-defined on

H^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})
.

Proof.

Suppose
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
and
(\mu_{1},\beta_{1},\psi_{1})
are cohomologous, and
(\mu_{2},\beta_{2},\theta_{2})\in\ker\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}
. Then, by the previous lemma we have

\langle(\beta_{1},\psi_{1})-(\beta,\psi),(\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\rangle~=~\langle D^{\prime}(\Phi(V)),(\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\rangle.

The Kähler identities and the holomorphicity condition twice gives

\langle D^{\prime}(\Phi(V)),(\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\rangle~=~\langle\Phi(V),i\Lambda D^{\prime\prime}(\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\rangle~=~\langle D^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(V)),\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu_{2}\rangle.

Using Remark
4.11
, we have

\langle(\beta_{1},\psi_{1})-(\beta,\psi),(\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\rangle~=~\langle\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi(\mu_{1}-\mu),\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu_{2}\rangle~.

Hence,

\langle(\mu_{1},\beta_{1},\theta_{1})-(\mu,\beta,\theta),(\mu_{2},\beta_{2},\theta_{2})\rangle_{h_{0}}=0
.
∎

4.7.
Constructing a local slice

Proposition 4.19
.

Fix
p\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
and let
x\in\mathcal{C}(P)
be a lift of
p
that satisfies the Hitchin equation (
4.4
). Let
\mathrm{B}^{\bullet}
be the associated deformation complex from (
4.5
). Then there exists a complex submanifold
\mathcal{S}_{x}
of
F^{-1}(0)
which passes through
x
and satisfies the following:

(1)

\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{S}_{x}=\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})
;

(2)

\mathcal{S}_{x}
is biholomorphic to a neighborhood of
0
in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})
;

(3)

if
g\in\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
and
y=gx

is another lift of
p
solving the Hitchin equations, then

\mathcal{S}_{y}=g\mathcal{S}_{x}
; and

(4)

if
x
is regularly stable, then the action map

\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)\times\mathcal{S}_{x}\to F^{-1}(0)

is a diffeomorphism onto an open neighborhood of
x
.

Proof.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
be large enough such that
h_{s}
is positive definite on
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
and consider the Hodge decomposition

\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}=\mathrm{Im}(\delta_{B}^{0})\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})\oplus\mathrm{Im}((\delta_{B}^{1})^{*})~.

This decomposition is orthogonal with respect to
h_{s}
and
\mathrm{I}
-invariant since both
F^{k}
and the orbit
\Aut_{0}(P)^{k+1}\cdotp x
are holomorphic. Given neighborhoods
U
and
V^{k}
of the origin in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})
and
\mathrm{Im}((\delta_{B}^{1})^{*})
, respectively, we consider the holomorphic map

\Psi^{k}:=F^{k}\circ\left({\exp^{k}_{x}}_{\big|U\oplus V^{s}}\right):U\oplus V^{k}\longrightarrow\Omega^{2}_{b}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{k-1}~.

Using that
\mathrm{d}_{0}\exp^{k}_{x}
is the identity, we obtain that
\mathrm{d}_{(0,0)}\Psi^{k}
is the restriction of
\delta_{B}^{1}
to
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet})\oplus\mathrm{Im}((\delta_{B}^{1})^{*})
. In particular, its restriction to the second factor is a linear isomorphism. By the implicit function theorem for complex Hilbert manifolds, up to shrinking
U
if necessary, there exists a unique holomorphic map
\varphi^{k}:U\to V^{k}
such that for
(u,v)\in U\times V^{k}
we have
\Psi^{k}(u,v)=0
if and only if
v=\varphi^{k}(u)
.

Lemma 4.20
.

The map
\varphi^{k}
described above takes value in
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{C}(P)
(in particular it is independent of
k
and will be denoted
\varphi
). Moreover, it satisfies
\mathrm{d}_{0}\varphi=0

Proof.

For the first item, observe that doing the same construction with the Sobolev completion
W^{k+1,2}
instead of
W^{k,2}
yields a map
\varphi^{k+1}:U\to V^{k+1}
such that for
(u,v)\in U\times V^{k+1}
we have
\Psi^{k+1}(u,v)=0
if and only if
v=\varphi^{k+1}(u)
.
Nevertheless,
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k+1}
is contained in
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
, so
V^{k+1}=V^{k}\cap\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{C}(P)^{k+1}
and
\Psi^{k+1}
is the restriction of
\Psi^{k}
to
U\oplus V^{k+1}
.
Hence, for all
u\in U
we have

\Psi^{k+1}(u,\varphi^{k+1}(u))=\Psi^{k}(u,\varphi^{k+1}(u))~,

so the uniqueness part of the implicit function theorem gives
\varphi^{k+1}(u)=\varphi^{k}(u)
. In particular,
\varphi^{k}
takes value in
\bigcap_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}V^{k+\alpha}
which is equal to
V^{k}\cap\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{C}(P)
.

To compute
\mathrm{d}_{0}\varphi
, we differentiate the equation
\Psi^{k}(u,\varphi(u))=0
. It gives

\mathrm{d}^{1}_{(0,0)}\Psi^{k}+\mathrm{d}^{2}_{(0,0)}\Psi^{k}\circ\mathrm{d}_{0}\varphi=0

where
\mathrm{d}^{1}\Psi^{k}
and
\mathrm{d}^{2}\Psi^{k}
are respectively the differential of
\Psi^{k}
with respect to the first and second variable, and so their value at
(0,0)
coincides with the restriction of
\delta_{B}^{1}
to
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet})
and
\mathrm{Im}((\delta_{B}^{1})^{*})
respectively. Since
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet})
is contained in
\ker(\delta_{B}^{1})
, the above equation gives
\mathrm{d}_{0}\varphi=0
.
∎

Consider now the immersion

(4.8)

\sigma:U\subset\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}~:~u\longmapsto\exp_{x}(u,\varphi(u))\ ,

and let
\mathcal{S}_{x}=\sigma(U)
. The above lemma and discussion imply that

\mathcal{S}_{x}
is a holomorphic submanifold of
\mathcal{C}(P)
contained in

F^{-1}(0)
and passing through
x
with
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{S}_{x}=\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet})
. Furthermore, if
y=gx
is another
lift of
p
solving the Hitchin equation, then
g\in\Aut_{0}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
and
so preserves the harmonic metric. By the uniqueness part of the implicit
function theorem, we easily obtain that
\sigma_{y}=g\sigma_{x}
, and so
\mathcal{S}_{y}=g\mathcal{S}_{x}
.

Lemma 4.21
.

If
x
is regularly stable, up to shrinking
\mathcal{S}_{x}
if necessary, the
action of
\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)^{k+1}
on
\mathcal{S}_{x}
defines a map

\wp^{k}:\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)^{k+1}\times\mathcal{S}_{x}\longrightarrow(F^{k})^{-1}(0)~:~(g,y)\longmapsto g\cdotp y\ ,

which is a diffeomorphism onto an open neighborhood of
x
in
(F^{k})^{-1}(0)
.

Proof.

First note that since
\mathcal{S}_{x}
is contained in
\mathcal{C}(P)
, the map
\wp^{k}
is smooth.
Moreover, its differential at
(\mathrm{Id},x)
is given by

(\delta_{B}^{0},\iota):\aut(P)^{k}\times\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{A}^{\bullet})\to\ker(\delta_{B}^{1})~,

where
\iota
is the natural injection. Using the decomposition

\ker(\delta_{B}^{1})=\mathrm{Im}(\delta_{B}^{0})\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\bullet})
, we get
that
D_{(\mathrm{Id},x)}\wp^{k}
is a linear isomorphism.
By the inverse function theorem, up to shrinking
\mathcal{S}_{x}
,
there exists a neighborhood
W
of
\mathrm{Id}
in
\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)^{k+1}

such that
\wp^{k}
restricts to a diffeomorphism from
W\times\mathcal{S}_{x}
onto an open neighborhood of
x
in
(F^{k})^{-1}(0)
.

The only thing that remains to be proved is that, up to shrinking
\mathcal{S}_{x}

if necessary, then for any
y\in\mathcal{S}_{x}
its
\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)^{k+1}
-orbit
does not intersect
\mathcal{S}_{x}
at a different point. Assume to the contrary
that this is false. Then there are sequences

(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{S}_{x}
and
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in

\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)^{k+1}
such that
(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
x

and
(g_{n}y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of
\mathcal{S}_{x}
converging to

x
. By Proposition
4.8
, the action of

\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)^{k+1}
on
\mathcal{C}(P)^{k}
is proper, so the sequence

(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
subconverges to some
g\in\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)^{k+1}
. In
particular,
gx=x
, and so
g=\mathrm{Id}
since the action is free
on regularly stable Higgs bundles (Proposition
4.8
).
But this implies that
g_{n}\in W
for
n
large enough, contradicting
the fact that
\wp^{k}
is a diffeomorphism.
∎

The proof now follows from Proposition
4.8
: if

y\in\mathcal{C}(P)
and
g\in\overline{\Aut}_{0}(P)^{k+1}
is such that
gy\in\mathcal{C}(P)
, then

g\in\mathcal{G}(P)
.
∎

4.8.
Proof of Theorem
4.6

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

Let us first prove the theorem on the open set
\mathbf{M}^{rs}(\mathsf{G})
corresponding to regularly stable Higgs bundles. In this case, the slices constructed in
Proposition
4.19
form a smooth atlas for which the quotient
map
\pi:F^{-1}(0)^{rs}\to\mathbf{M}^{rs}(\mathsf{G})
is a smooth principal
\Aut_{0}(P)
-bundle. To prove this atlas is holomorphic, the
key remark is that the almost complex structure
\mathrm{I}
on
F^{-1}(0)^{rs}

naturally induces an almost complex structure (still denoted by
\mathrm{I}
)
on
\mathbf{M}^{rs}(\mathsf{G})
as follow: given a tangent vector
u\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathbf{M}^{rs}(\mathsf{G})
, define

\mathrm{I}_{p}(u):=\mathrm{d}_{x}\pi(\mathrm{I}_{x}(v))~,

where
v\in\mathrm{T}_{x}F^{-1}(0)
is such that
\mathrm{d}_{x}\pi(v)=u
. Since
\mathrm{I}
is
\Aut_{0}(P)^{k}
-invariant and
\mathrm{I}
preserves vertical vectors, one easily checks that the above definition is independent of the choice of the lift. Moreover, the restriction of
\pi
to the slices is holomorphic. Since
\mathrm{I}
is integrable on
\mathcal{C}(P)
(by the exponential maps), it is on the slices, and hence on
\mathbf{M}^{rs}(\mathsf{G})
. Furthermore, the transition functions for nearby slices are holomorphic, defining a holomorphic atlas on
\mathbf{M}^{rs}(\mathsf{G})
.

For points lifting to stable but not regularly stable Higgs
bundles
x
in
F^{-1}(0)
, the complex submanifold
\mathcal{S}_{x}
of
Proposition
4.19
is invariant under the stabilizer of
x

in
\Aut_{0}(P)
(by item (3)). Since this stabilizer is finite, we get an orbifold structure on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
.

To see that the holomorphic bundle map
\Theta:\pi^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\to\ker(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\mathcal{J}})
from §
3.2
descends to
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
, it suffices to check
\Theta_{(J,\Phi)}(\mu)
is tangent to
F^{-1}(0)
for each
(J,\Phi)\in F^{-1}(0)
and each
\mu\in\pi^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma).
This is equivalent to
\Theta_{(J,\Phi)}(\mu)
being in the kernel of the boundary map
\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}
from Lemma
4.9
. By Remark
3.5
, we have

\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}\left(\Theta_{(J,\Phi)}(\mu)\right)=\delta_{\mathrm{B}}^{1}\left(0,\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu,0\right)=D^{\prime\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu,0\right)=\left[\Phi,\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right]=0.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the hermitian form
h_{s}
descends to
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
via

h_{s}(u,v)_{p}:=h_{s}(\hat{u},\hat{v})_{x}

where
\hat{u}
and
\hat{v}
are harmonic vectors lifting
u
and
v
. Since
h_{s}
is
\Aut_{0}(P_{\mathsf{K}})
-invariant, such a definition is independent of the choice of
x
lifting
p
(as soon as
x
satisfies the Hitchin equations). The corresponding
2
-form is closed by Corollary
4.15
.

For
s=0
, it suffices to define
h_{0}
on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
by evaluating along semiharmonic lift: by Proposition
4.18
, it defines a hermitian form on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
.
∎

Remark 4.22
.

It is important to note that if
\mu\in\mathrm{T}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
corresponds to
a trivial deformation of
X
, then
\Theta(\mu)\in\mathrm{Im}D^{\prime\prime}
. However,
it may be the case that
\Theta(\mu)\in\mathrm{Im}D^{\prime\prime}
even for nontrivial
classes in
H^{1}(X,T_{X})
.

4.9.
Nonabelian Hodge and variation of the metric

We end this section by proving two important propositions concerning the regularity of the nonabelian Hodge map and the first variation of the metric along the slice.

The
\mathsf{G}
-character variety
of the surface
\Sigma
is the affine GIT quotient

\Hom(\pi_{1}\Sigma,\mathsf{G})\sslash\mathsf{G}

of the space of group homomorphisms
\pi_{1}\Sigma\to\mathsf{G}
by conjugation (see

[
63
]
, and also
[
79
]
and the references therein).
As in the introduction we denote the irreducible locus of the character variety
by
\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
,

(4.9)

\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})\subset\Hom(\pi_{1}\Sigma,\mathsf{G})\sslash\mathsf{G}.

The holonomy map
\mathsf{hol}
from the moduli space of irreducible
flat
\mathsf{G}
-connections to
\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
is a complex analytic isomorphism usually called the
Riemann-Hilbert correspondence
.

Recall that the map
\mathrm{H}
from (
3.5
) sends a Higgs bundle
(J,\Phi)
to the
\mathsf{G}
-connection
A_{J}+\frac{1}{2i}(\Phi-\Phi^{*})
, and that
\mathrm{H}(J,\Phi)
is flat if
(J,\Phi)

solves the Hitchin equations (
4.13
).
Since
\mathrm{H}
is equivariant with respect to the action of
\Aut(P_{\mathsf{K}})
, it
descends to a map from the joint moduli space
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
to the moduli
space of flat
\mathsf{G}
-connections. Post-composing with the (Riemann-Hilbert) holonomy map
\mathsf{hol}
defines a map from the joint moduli space
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
to
\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
which we also denote by
\mathrm{H}

(4.10)

\mathrm{H}:\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\longrightarrow\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})~:~[J,\Phi]\longmapsto\mathsf{hol}([A_{J}+\tfrac{1}{2i}(\Phi-\Phi^{*})])\ ,

and which we call the
nonabelian Hodge map
.
By work of Hitchin
[
47
]
, Simpson
[
81
]
,
Donaldson
[
29
]
and Corlette
[
26
]
, the
restriction
\mathrm{H}_{X}:\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})\to{\bf X}(\mathsf{G})
is a real analytic
diffeomorphism (in the orbifold sense) for each
X\in{\bf T}(\Sigma)
.
According to
[
84
, Theorem 7.18]
,
the map

{\bf M}(\mathsf{G})\longrightarrow{\bf T}(\Sigma)\times{\bf X}(\mathsf{G})~:~[J,\Phi]\longmapsto([j],\mathrm{H}[J,\Phi])

is a homeomorphism.
As expected, the relative nonabelian Hodge map is real
analytic, at least on the stable locus. For a closely related result,
see Slegers
[
87
]
.
In the following, we assume a fixed real analytic structure on the
underlying principal bundle
P\to\Sigma
.

Theorem 4.23
.

The nonabelian Hodge map
\mathrm{H}:\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
is real
analytic.

Proof.

Fix a point
x\in\mathcal{C}(P)
corresponding to a point
p\in{\bf M}(\mathsf{G})
.
We assume
x=(J_{0},\Phi_{0})
satisfies (
4.4
).
Recall from §
2.1.3
that a structure group reduction to a
maximal compact
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
is equivalent to a Cartan involution

\tau
. Let
\tau_{0}
denote a harmonic metric for
(J_{0},\Phi_{0})
.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{x}
be the slice obtained in Proposition
4.19
.
By Theorem
4.5
of Hitchin and Simpson, for each

(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{S}_{x}
there is a unique harmonic metric
\tau
satisfying
(
4.4
).
We may therefore view
\tau
as a map
\tau:\mathcal{S}_{x}\times P\to\End(\mathfrak{g})
.
We first claim that
\tau
is real analytic. To see this,
recall that we may express
\tau=\Ad_{g^{-1}}\tau_{0}\Ad_{g}
for some
g
.
Let
k=(\Ad_{g})^{\ast}\Ad_{g}
, where the adjoint is with respect to the
hermitian structure on
P(\mathfrak{g})
defined by
\tau_{0}
(see §
2.1.3
). Analyticity of

k
implies that of
\tau
; indeed,
\tau=\tau_{0}k
. Now
for each
(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{S}_{x}
, Equation (
4.4
)
in the adjoint representation becomes

(4.11)

\bar{\partial}_{A_{J}^{0}}(k^{-1}\partial_{A_{J}^{0}}k)+F_{A_{J}^{0}}+[\Phi,k^{-1}\Phi^{\ast}k]=0\ ,

where
A_{J}^{0}
is the Chern-Singer connection with respect to

(J,\tau_{0})
.
By the continuity of
\rm H
, we may assume
k
is uniformly invertible.
Then in local coordinates and trivializations, (
4.11
)
is a second order nonlinear
elliptic system in
k
. Moreover, since the map
\sigma
from (
4.8
)
defining the slice is holomorphic, in particular real
analytic,
and since
A_{J}^{0}
is analytic in
J
,
the coefficients of (
4.11
) are real analytic
on
\mathcal{S}_{x}
. By a theorem of Morrey
[
68
]
,
k
(and therefore also
\tau
) is
real analytic on
\mathcal{S}_{x}\times P
.
Next, from the analyticity of
\tau
it
follows that the Chern-Singer connection
A_{J}
associated to
(J,\tau)
is real
analytic as well. Hence, the family

D_{(J,\Phi)}=A_{J}+\tfrac{1}{2i}(\Phi+\tau(\Phi))

of flat connections is real analytic on
\mathcal{S}_{x}\times P
. Finally, from the
analyticity of the Riemann-Hilbert map, the result follows.
∎

We now show that the first variation of the metric solving the Hitchin equations is zero along the slice associated to the semiharmonics.
The following theorem generalizes

[
54
, Theorem 3.5.1]
and
[
25
, Proposition 3.12]

to the setting of the joint moduli space.

Theorem 4.24
.

Fix a Cartan involution
\tau
on
P
, and let
(J,\Phi)
be a stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs which solves the Hitchin equation for
\tau
. Suppose
(J_{t},\Phi_{t})
is a 1-parameter family of Higgs bundles with
(J,\Phi)=(J_{0},\Phi_{0})
which is contained in a
slice
\mathcal{S}_{(J,\Phi)}
from Proposition
4.19
.
As in the proof of Theorem
4.23
,
let
(\tau(t))
be the 1-parameter family of Cartan involutions such that
(J_{t},\Phi_{t})
solves the
\tau(t)
-Hitchin equation. Then

\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{dt}}\right|_{t=0}\tau_{t}=0.

Before proving the theorem, we prove an auxiliary lemma. Let
(J_{t})
and
(\tau_{t})
be 1-parameter families of complex structures on
P
and Cartan involutions, respectively, and denote the associated Chern-Singer connection by
A_{J_{t}}
. Set
\tau=\tau(0)
and
\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\tau}=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{dt}}\right|_{t=0}\tau(t)
.
Note that
\tau\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\tau}
is a derivation. Since
\mathfrak{g}
is semisimple there
is section
Z
of
P(\mathfrak{g})
with

\tau\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\tau}=\ad_{Z}
.

Lemma 4.25
.

With the above set up, we have

(4.12)

(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J})^{1,0}=-(\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\tau(Z))^{1,0}+\tau((\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J})^{0,1}).

Proof.

Let
X
be a smooth section of
P(\mathfrak{g})
, independent of the
parameter. By definition of the Chern-Singer
connection,
\mathrm{d}_{A_{J_{t}}}\tau_{t}(X)=\tau_{t}(\mathrm{d}_{A_{J_{t}}}X)
.
Differentiating this at
t=0
, we have

\displaystyle[\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J},\tau(X)]+\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\tau}(X)

\displaystyle=\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\tau}(\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}X)+\tau([\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J},X])

\displaystyle[\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J},\tau(X)]+\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}[\tau(Z),\tau(X)]

\displaystyle=[\tau(Z),\tau(\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}X)]+[\tau(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J}),\tau(X)]

\displaystyle[\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J}+\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\tau(Z),\tau(X)]

\displaystyle=[\tau(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J}),\tau(X)].

Since
X
was arbitrary,
\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J}=-\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\tau(Z)+\tau(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J})
. Taking the
(1,0)
-part completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.24
.

Let
(J_{t},\Phi_{t})
be a 1-parameter family in a semiharmonic slice
\mathcal{S}_{(J,\Phi)}
such that
(J_{0},\Phi_{0})=(J,\Phi)
and
\tau_{t}
be the
1-parameter family of Cartan involutions which such that
(J_{t},\Phi_{t})

solve the
\tau_{t}
-Hitchin equation

(4.13)

F_{A_{J_{t}}}-\tfrac{1}{4}[\Phi_{t},\tau_{t}(\Phi_{t})]=0.

Set
(\frac{1}{2i}\beta,\psi)=((\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J})^{0,1},(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Phi})^{1,0})
. By assumption
(\beta,\psi)
are semiharmonic, hence

(4.14)

0=\tfrac{1}{2i}D^{\prime}(\beta,\psi)=\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\beta\right)+\left[\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*},\tfrac{1}{2i}\psi\right]=\tfrac{1}{2i}\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\beta-\tfrac{1}{4}[\tau(\Phi),\psi].

We first compute the first variation of (
4.13
) at
t=0
. Set
\tau=\tau_{0}
.

\overset{\bullet}{\wideparen{\tau(\Phi)}}=\tau(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Phi})+\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\tau}(\Phi)=\tau(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Phi})+\tau(\tau\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\tau}(\Phi))=\tau(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Phi})+\tau([Z,\Phi]).

Since
\Phi
has type
(1,0)
, the
(0,1)
-part of
\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Phi}
does not enter in the computation, and we have

\overset{\bullet}{\wideparen{[\Phi,\tau(\Phi)]}}=[\psi,\tau(\Phi)]+[\Phi,\tau(\psi)]+[\Phi,[\tau(Z),\tau(\Phi)]].

For the curvature term, we have
\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{F}_{A_{J}}=\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J})
. Using (
4.12
), and
\tfrac{1}{2i}\beta=(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{A}_{J})^{0,1}
, we have

\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{F}_{A_{J}}=\tfrac{1}{2i}\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\beta+\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\left(\tau\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\beta\right)-(\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\tau(Z))^{1,0}\right)=\tfrac{1}{2i}\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\beta+\tau\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\beta\right)-\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}((\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\tau(Z))^{1,0}).

Hence, using
[\Phi,\tau(\psi)]=\tau([\tau(\Phi),\psi])
and (
4.14
), the linearization of the Hitchin equations (
4.13
) is given by

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2i}\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\beta-\tfrac{1}{4}[\tau(\Phi),\psi]+\tau\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\beta-\tfrac{1}{4}[\tau(\Phi),\psi]\right)-\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}((\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\tau(Z))^{1,0})-\tfrac{1}{4}[\Phi,[\tau(Z),\tau(\Phi)]]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}((\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\tau(Z))^{1,0})-\tfrac{1}{4}[\Phi,[\tau(Z),\tau(\Phi)]]

\displaystyle=-D^{\prime\prime}\left((\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\tau(Z))^{1,0}-\tfrac{1}{2i}[\tau(Z),\tau(\Phi)]\right)

\displaystyle=-D^{\prime\prime}D^{\prime}(\tau(Z)).

By the stability assumption, this implies
\tau(Z)=0
, completing the proof.
∎

5.
Isomonodromic and horizontal distributions and energy

Recall that
\omega_{0}
is the closed two form on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
defined by the

\mathrm{I}
-invariant part of the pullback of the Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form by the
nonabelian Hodge map (
4.10
).
The associated hermitian form
h_{0}
is given by

h_{0}(\cdot,\cdot)=2(\omega_{0}(\mathrm{I}\cdot,\cdot)+i\omega_{0}(\cdot,\cdot)).

In this section we prove Theorems
5.15
and
5.22
concerning the relation between the kernel of
h_{0},
the isomonodromic distribution and the energy. These are equivalent to Theorems B and C from the introduction.
We start by defining the isomonodromic distribution and deducing some immediate consequences.

5.1.
Horizontal and isomonodromic distribution

Recall that the hermitian form
h_{0}
is positive definite on the fibers of
\pi:\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
. Denote the vertical tangent bundle by

\mathrm{V}\mathbf{M}=\ker(\mathrm{d}\pi)\subset\mathrm{T}\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G}).

Definition 5.1
.

The
horizontal distribution
is the subbundle
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathrm{T}\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
which is
h_{0}
-perpendicular to the vertical tangent bundle. For each
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
and each tangent vector
[\mu]\in\mathrm{T}_{\pi(x)}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
, there is a unique lift
w_{[\mu]}\in\mathcal{H}_{x}
which we call the
horizontal vector
associated to
[\mu]
.

Note that the horizontal vector
w_{[\mu]}
is the lift of
[\mu]
with minimal
h_{0}
-norm.

The fibers of the nonabelian Hodge map
\mathrm{H}
define a foliation of
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
called the
isomonodromic foliation
. The leaves of the foliation are called
isomonodromic leaves
and will be denoted by
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}=\mathrm{H}^{-1}(\rho)
. The leaf
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
can also be interpreted as the image of a section

s_{\rho}:\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)\to\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G}).

Definition 5.2
.

The
isomonodromic distribution
is the subbundle
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathrm{T}\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
defined by

\mathcal{D}=\ker(\mathrm{d}\mathrm{H})
.
For each
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
and each tangent vector
[\mu]\in\mathrm{T}_{\pi(x)}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
, there is a unique lift
\ell_{[\mu]}\in\mathcal{D}_{x}
which we call the
isomonodromic vector
associated to
[\mu].

In the §
5.2
, we will prove the following proposition relating the holomorphic section
\Theta
from §
3.2
and Theorem
4.6
with the horizontal and isomonodromic distributions, see Remark
5.10
and Lemma
5.13
.

Proposition 5.3
.

For
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
and
[\mu]\in\mathrm{T}_{\pi(x)}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
, let
w_{[\mu]}
and
\ell_{[\mu]}
be the horizontal and isomonodromic lifts of
[\mu]
. Then

(1)

\|w_{[\mu]}\|^{2}_{h_{0}}=-\|\Theta_{x}([\mu])\|^{2}_{h_{0}}
, and

(2)

w_{[\mu]}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell_{[\mu]}-\mathrm{I}\ell_{[i\mu]}\right)
.

The following two lemmas are immediate from the definition of
\omega_{0}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
and
\mathcal{D}_{x}\subset\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
be the isomonodromic distribution at
x.
Then
\mathcal{D}_{x}\cap\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{D}_{x})
is contained in the kernel of
\omega_{0}
and hence the kernel of
h_{0}.

Proof.

Suppose
\ell\in\mathcal{D}_{x}\cap\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{D}_{x})
. For any tangent vector
u\in T_{x}\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
we have

\omega_{0}(\ell,u)=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{H}^{*}\omega_{ABG}(\ell,u)+\mathrm{H}^{*}\omega_{ABG}(\mathrm{I}(\ell),\mathrm{I}(u)))=0

since
\ell
and
I(\ell)
are in the kernel of
\mathrm{d}_{x}\mathrm{H}
.
∎

We will prove the kernel of
\omega_{0}
at
x
is exactly
\mathcal{D}_{x}\cap\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{D}_{x})
in the next subsection.

Lemma 5.5
.

Both isomonodromic distribution
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{D})
are isotropic with respect to the real part of
h_{0}
. In particular, for any isomonodromic vector
\ell\in\mathcal{D}
we have

\|\ell\|_{h_{0}}^{2}=0
.

Proof.

The real part of
h_{0}
is
2\omega_{0}(\mathrm{I}\cdot,\cdot)
. For
\ell,u\in\mathcal{D}
we have

2\omega_{0}(\mathrm{I}(\ell),u)=\mathrm{H}^{*}\omega_{ABG}(\mathrm{I}(\ell),u)+\mathrm{H}^{*}\omega_{ABG}(-\ell,\mathrm{I}(u))=0

since
\ell
and
u
are in the kernel of
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{H}.
The proof is the same for
\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{D}).

∎

Remark 5.6
.

Lemma
5.5
implies that
h_{0}
is nonpositive on the horizontal distribution
\mathcal{H}
since
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{H}
are both transverse to the vertical bundle
\mathrm{V}\mathbf{M}
and
h_{0}
is positive definite on
\mathrm{V}\mathbf{M}
.

5.2.
Semiharmonic representatives

For this section, we fix a stable Higgs bundle
(J,\Phi)
solving the Hitchin equation and let
x=[J,\Phi]
be the associated point in
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
. We now relate the semiharmonic representatives of horizontal vectors and isomonodromic vectors.

Recall that the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
can be represented by
the space of semiharmonic tangent vectors at
(J,\Phi)
.
The nonabelian Hodge map on the configuration space is defined by

\mathrm{H}(J,\Phi)=A_{J}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi+\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*}.

As before, we write this flat connection as
D=\mathrm{H}(J,\Phi)
and decompose
D=D^{\prime\prime}+D^{\prime}
, where

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 31.91608pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.91608pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime\prime}=\bar{\partial}_{J}+\frac{1}{2i}\Phi}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 55.91608pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 102.02722pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime}=\partial_{A_{J}}+\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Now
D^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}
satisfy the Kähler identities
(
4.7
), and since
D
is flat we have
D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}=-D^{\prime\prime}D^{\prime}
.
As in Definition
4.16
, a tangent vector
(\mu,\beta,\psi)\in\mathrm{T}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathcal{C}(P)
is called semiharmonic if

(5.1)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 57.51382pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-57.51382pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)+D^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)=0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 81.51382pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 127.62497pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime}(\beta,\psi)=0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

.
Finally, recall that the
h_{0}
-norm of a class
v=[(\mu,\beta,\psi)]\in\mathrm{T}\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
is

\|v\|^{2}_{h_{0}}=-\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}+\|(\beta,\psi)\|^{2}~,

where
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is any semiharmonic representative of

v
(see Proposition
4.18
), and where henceforth

\|\cdot\|

(unannotated) always denotes the
L^{2}
-norm.
At
(J,\Phi)
, the section
\Theta
from §
3.2
associates to a Beltrami differential
\mu
the vertical vector

\Theta_{(J,\Phi)}(\mu)=(0,\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu,0).

Note that
\Theta_{(J,\Phi)}(\mu)
is not usually harmonic. Its
harmonic representative is

{\rm pr}_{\ker(D^{\prime})}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)
, where
{\rm pr}_{\ker(D^{\prime})}
is the

L^{2}
-orthogonal projection onto
\ker(D^{\prime}).

By holomorphicity of
\Phi
, this only depends on the class
[\mu]
of
\mu
.
The following proposition is immediate.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
and
[\mu]\in\mathrm{T}_{\pi(x)}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
be a tangent vector. Then

\|\Theta_{x}([\mu])\|_{h_{0}}^{2}=\|{\rm pr}_{\ker(D^{\prime})}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\|^{2},

where
(J,\Phi)
is a stable Higgs bundle solving the Hitchin equation with
x=[J,\Phi].

5.2.1.
Semiharmonic horizontal vectors

We have the following lemmas characterizing the semiharmonic horizontal vectors and their norms.

Lemma 5.8
.

Fix a Beltrami differential
\mu,
and suppose
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
satisfies

D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)+D^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)=0
. Then
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is
the semiharmonic representative of the horizontal vector

w_{[\mu]}\in\mathcal{H}_{x}
if and only there exists a unique

\eta\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
such that
D^{\prime}(\eta)=(\beta,\psi)
.
In this case,
D^{\prime\prime}(\eta)={\rm pr}_{\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right),

where
{\rm pr}_{\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})}
denotes the orthogonal projection of onto
\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})
.

Proof.

If
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is a semiharmonic horizontal vector then it
satisfies
D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)+D^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)=0
and is

h_{0}
-perpendicular to all vertical vectors. But vertical vectors are
given by triples
(0,\beta_{1},\psi_{1})
with
(\beta_{1},\psi_{1})\in\ker(D^{\prime\prime})
. Thus,

0=\langle(\mu,\beta,\psi),(0,\beta_{1},\psi_{1})\rangle_{h_{0}}=\langle(\beta,\psi),(\beta_{1},\psi_{1})\rangle~

implies
(\beta,\psi)\in\ker(D^{\prime\prime})^{\perp}=\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime}).
So there exists
\eta\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
such that
D^{\prime}\eta=(\beta,\psi)
. Uniqueness of
\eta
follows from the stability assumption.
Conversely, if such an
\eta
exists, then
(\beta,\psi)
is
h_{0}
-perpendicular to the vertical space and semiharmonic since
D^{\prime 2}=0
. Hence
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
represents a horizontal vector
w_{[\mu]}\in\mathcal{H}.

Now suppose
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is a semiharmonic horizontal vector, and
write
(\beta,\psi)=D^{\prime}\eta.
It remains to prove
D^{\prime\prime}\eta={\rm pr}_{\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})}(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu).

The
D^{\prime\prime}
and
D^{\prime}
Laplacians on
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
are given by

(D^{\prime\prime})^{*}D^{\prime\prime}
and
(D^{\prime})^{*}D^{\prime}
, respectively. By the stability assumption,
both of these operators have trivial kernel. Let
G_{D^{\prime\prime}}
and
G_{D^{\prime}}

be the associated Green’s functions (i.e. bounded two-sided inverses)
of the respective Laplacians.
Using
D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}=-D^{\prime\prime}D^{\prime}
, one checks that
G_{D^{\prime}}=G_{D^{\prime\prime}}.

Since
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is semiharmonic, we have
D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)=-D^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)
. By the Kähler identities, we can write
\eta
as

\eta=G_{D^{\prime}}(D^{\prime})^{*}D^{\prime}\eta=G_{D^{\prime}}(D^{\prime})^{*}(\beta,\psi)=-iG_{D^{\prime}}\Lambda D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)=G_{D^{\prime\prime}}(D^{\prime\prime})^{*}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right).

Using the Hodge decomposition, write
\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu=D^{\prime\prime}u+v
, where
v\in\ker(D^{\prime\prime})^{\ast}
. Then

(5.2)

D^{\prime\prime}\eta=D^{\prime\prime}G_{D^{\prime\prime}}(D^{\prime\prime})^{*}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)=D^{\prime\prime}G_{D^{\prime\prime}}(D^{\prime\prime})^{*}(D^{\prime\prime}u)=D^{\prime\prime}u={\rm pr}_{\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right).

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is a horizontal semiharmonic vector. Then

(1)

\|(\beta,\psi)\|^{2}=\|{\rm pr}_{\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\|^{2}
, and

(2)

\|(\mu,\beta,\psi)\|_{h_{0}}^{2}=-\|{\rm pr}_{\ker(D^{\prime})}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\|^{2},

where
{\rm pr}_{\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})}
and
{\rm pr}_{\ker(D^{\prime})}
are the orthogonal projections onto
\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})
and
\ker(D^{\prime})
, respectively.

Remark 5.10
.

Combining Item (2) of Lemma
5.9
with Proposition
5.7
gives the proof of Item (1) of Proposition
5.3
.

Proof.

Let
(\mu,\beta_{\mu},\psi_{\mu})
is a horizontal semiharmonic vector and
\eta\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
be as in Lemma
5.8
. Then, using the Kähler identities and
D^{\prime\prime}(\beta_{\mu},\psi_{\mu})=-D^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)
, we have

\|(\beta_{\mu},\psi_{\mu})\|^{2}=\langle D^{\prime}\eta,D^{\prime}\eta\rangle=\langle\eta,(D^{\prime})^{*}D^{\prime}\eta\rangle=\langle\eta,-i\Lambda D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\rangle=\langle D^{\prime\prime}\eta,\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\rangle.

Item (1) now follows from the fact that
D^{\prime\prime}\eta={\rm pr}_{\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})}(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu).

Item (2) follows from Item (1) and the fact that
\ker(D^{\prime})=\ker((D^{\prime\prime})^{*})
. Namely,

\displaystyle\|(\mu,\beta_{\mu},\psi_{\mu})\|_{h_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}+\|(\beta_{\mu},\psi_{\mu})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}+\|{\rm pr}_{\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\|{\rm pr}_{\ker(D^{\prime})}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\|^{2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

5.2.2.
Semiharmonic isomonodromic vectors

We now characterize semiharmonic representatives of the isomonodromic distribution. We refer to these as semiharmonic isomonodromic vectors.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
D=\mathrm{H}(J,\Phi)
be the flat connection associated to
(J,\Phi).
Fix a Beltrami differential
\mu,
and suppose
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
satisfies the holomorphicity condition
D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)+D^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)=0
.
Then,
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is a representative of the isomonodromic vector
\ell_{[\mu]}\in\mathcal{D}
if and only if there exists a unique
\zeta\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
such that

\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathrm{H}(\mu,\beta,\psi)=D\zeta.

Proof.

A tangent vector
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
defines an isomonodromic vector if and only if the
\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathrm{H}(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is zero when projected to the moduli space of flat connections. This is equivalent to being in the image of the flat connection
D.
Uniqueness follows from the assumption that
(J,\Phi)
is stable.
∎

From the computation of the derivative of
\mathrm{H}
in Lemma
3.8
,
and using Theorem
4.24
,
the equation
\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathrm{H}(\mu,\beta,\psi)=D\zeta
is written explicitly as

(5.3)

\bar{\partial}_{J}\zeta+\left[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi,\zeta\right]+\partial_{A_{J}}\zeta+\left[\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*},\zeta\right]=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\beta+\psi+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu+\beta^{\ast}-\psi^{\ast}-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right).

Lemma 5.12
.

Suppose
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is a semiharmonic isomonodromic vector, and
let
\zeta
be the unique solution to

\mathrm{d}_{(J,\Phi)}\mathrm{H}(\mu,\beta,\psi)=D\zeta
. Then
\zeta=\zeta^{*}
, and

(5.4)

\textstyle{D^{\prime}\zeta=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\beta+\psi-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right)}
and
\textstyle{D^{\prime\prime}\zeta=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\beta^{*}-\psi^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right).}

Proof.

Suppose
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is a semiharmonic isomonodromic vector, then we have

D\zeta=D^{\prime}\zeta+D^{\prime\prime}\zeta=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\beta+\beta^{*}+\psi-\psi^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right).

Using
(D^{\prime})^{2}=0
and
D^{\prime}(\beta,\psi)=0
, we have

D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}\zeta=D^{\prime}\left(-\psi^{*},\beta^{*}\right)+D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)-D^{\prime}\left(\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right).

The term
D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}
vanishes since

D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}=\left[\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*},\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right]=0.

For the term
D^{\prime}\left(-\psi^{*},\beta^{*}\right),
we have

D^{\prime}\left(-\psi^{*},\beta^{*}\right)=-\partial_{A_{J}}+[\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*},\beta^{*}]=-(\bar{\partial}_{J}\psi+\left[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi,\beta\right])^{*}=-(D^{\prime\prime}(\beta,\psi)^{*}=D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*},

where we used the holomorphicity condition in the last equality. Hence, we have

D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}\zeta=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}+D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\right).

From this expression and the fact that
D^{\prime\prime}D^{\prime}=-D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}
, we conclude

D^{\prime\prime}D^{\prime}\zeta=(D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}\zeta)^{*}.

On the other hand, one checks that
(D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}\zeta)^{*}=D^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime}\zeta^{*}
for any
\zeta\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
. Hence,

D^{\prime\prime}D^{\prime}(\zeta-\zeta^{*})=0.

Since
\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime})=\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})^{*}
and stability implies
\ker(D^{\prime})=0
, we conclude
\zeta=\zeta^{*}
, as desired.

We now deduce the expressions for
D^{\prime}\zeta
and
D^{\prime\prime}\zeta.
The
(1,0)
part of (
5.3
) is

\partial_{A_{J}}\zeta+\left[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi,\zeta\right]=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\beta^{*}+\psi-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right)

Using
\zeta=\zeta^{*}
, we have

\partial_{A_{J}}\zeta-\left[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi,\zeta\right]=\partial_{A_{J}}\zeta^{*}-[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi,\zeta^{*}]=\left(\bar{\partial}_{J}\zeta+\left[\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*},\zeta\right]\right)^{*}=-\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\beta^{*}-\psi+\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right).

Combining these two equations gives

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 52.3334pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-52.3334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\partial_{A_{J}}\zeta=\tfrac{1}{2i}(\psi-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*})}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 76.3334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 122.44455pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\left[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi,\zeta\right]=\tfrac{1}{2i}\beta^{*}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

It follows that
\left[\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*},\zeta\right]=\tfrac{1}{2i}\beta
, and we conclude

D^{\prime}\zeta=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\beta+\psi-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right),

as desired. The formula for
D^{\prime\prime}\zeta
follows from a similar computation.
∎

5.2.3.
Relating the semiharmonic vectors

We now use Lemmas
5.8
and
5.12
to relate the semiharmonic isomonodromic and horizontal vectors.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
be a semiharmonic horizontal vector, and
(\mu,\beta_{1},\psi_{1})
and
(i\mu,\beta_{2},\psi_{2})
be semiharmonic isomonodromic vectors, then

(\beta,\psi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\left((\beta_{1},\psi_{1})-i(\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\right).

In particular,

(\mu,\beta,\psi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\left((\mu,\beta_{1},\psi_{1})-\mathrm{I}(i\mu,\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\right).

Proof.

Let
(\mu,\beta_{1},\psi_{1})
and
(i\mu,\beta_{2},\psi_{2})
be semiharmonic isomonodromic vectors. Consider the vector

w=(\mu,\tfrac{1}{2}(\beta_{1}-i\beta_{2}),\tfrac{1}{2}(\psi_{1}-i\psi_{2}))=\tfrac{1}{2}\left((\mu,\beta_{1},\psi_{1})-\mathrm{I}(i\mu,\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\right).

By Lemma
5.12
, there are hermitian sections
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
such that

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 61.7404pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-61.7404pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime}\zeta_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\beta_{1}+\psi_{1}-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right)}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 85.7404pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 131.85155pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime}\zeta_{2}=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\beta_{2}+\psi_{2}-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi i\mu\right)^{*}\right)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Hence

D^{\prime}(i\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{2})=\tfrac{1}{2}\left((\beta_{1},\psi_{1})-i(\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\right).

Since
w
is semiharmonic and
\tfrac{1}{2}\left((\beta_{1},\psi_{1})-i(\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\right)
is in the image of
D^{\prime}
, Lemma
5.8
implies
w
is a semiharmonic horizontal vector.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

Using the same notation as the above lemma, note that

(5.5)

D^{\prime\prime}\left(i\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2}\right)=-\beta^{*}+\psi^{*}.

Indeed,
-\beta^{*}+\psi^{*}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\beta_{1}^{*}+i\beta_{2}^{*}+\psi_{1}^{*}+i\psi_{2}^{*})
and

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 59.59451pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-59.59451pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime\prime}\zeta_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(-\beta_{1}^{*}-\psi_{1}^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 83.59451pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 129.70566pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime\prime}\zeta_{2}=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(-\beta_{2}^{*}-\psi_{2}^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi i\mu\right)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

5.3.
Proof of Theorem
B

The following is equivalent to Theorem
B
from the introduction.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{D}
be the horizontal and isomonodromic distributions on the joint moduli space
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
, respectively, and
\Theta:\pi^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{V}\mathbf{M}
be the holomorphic bundle map from Theorem
4.6
. Then the hermitian form
h_{0}
is nonpositive on
\mathcal{H}
, and for each
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
the kernel
\mathcal{K}_{x}
of
h_{0}
is given by

\mathcal{K}_{x}=\{w\in\mathcal{H}~|~\|w\|^{2}_{h_{0}}=0\}=\mathcal{D}_{x}\cap\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{D}_{x})=\ker(\Theta),

where in the last equality
\mathcal{H}
has been identified with
\pi^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
via
\mathrm{d}\pi
.
In particular, if
h_{0}
is nondegenerate at
x
then it has signature
(\dim\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G}),3g-3).

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
and a horizontal vector
w\in\mathcal{H}
. Let
(J,\Phi)
be a Higgs bundle solving the Hitchin equations with
x=[(J,\Phi)]
and
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
be a semiharmonic tangent vector for
(J,\Phi).
By Item (2) of Lemma
5.9
, we have

\|w\|^{2}_{h_{0}}:=\|(\mu,\beta,\psi)\|^{2}_{h_{0}}=-\|{\rm pr}_{\ker(D^{\prime})}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\|^{2}\leq 0.

Hence,
h_{0}
is nonpositive on the horizontal distribution and

\mathcal{K}_{x}=\{w\in\mathcal{H}~|~\|w\|^{2}_{h_{0}}=0\}~.

The signature of
h_{0}
at points where it is nondegenerate follows immediately.

It remains to prove
\mathcal{K}_{x}=\mathcal{D}_{x}\cap\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{D}_{x})
. The inclusion
\mathcal{D}_{x}\cap\mathrm{I}(\mathcal{D}_{x})\subset\mathcal{K}_{x}
is proven in Lemma
5.4
. We now prove the opposite inclusion. Since
\mathcal{K}_{x}
is complex, it suffices to prove
\mathcal{K}_{x}\subset\mathcal{D}_{x}.

Suppose
w\in\mathcal{K}_{x}
. By the above,
w
is in the horizontal distribution and has
\|w\|^{2}_{h_{0}}=0.

Hence, by Lemmas
5.9
and
5.8
,
\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\in\mathrm{Im}(D^{\prime\prime})
and there is a unique
\eta\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
such that

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 30.02666pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-30.02666pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime}\eta=\beta+\psi}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 54.02666pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 100.1378pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D^{\prime\prime}\eta=\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Since
\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu
is a
(0,1)
-form, we have
\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu=\bar{\partial}_{J}\eta
and
[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi,\eta]=0
. Hence,

D^{\prime}(\eta^{*})=\partial_{A_{J}}\eta^{*}+[\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right),\eta^{*}]=(\bar{\partial}_{J}\eta)^{*}=\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}.

Similarly, since
\beta
and
\psi
have type
(0,1)
and
(1,0)
, respectively, we have
\beta=[\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*},\eta]
and
\psi=\partial_{A_{J}}\eta
.
Thus,
\beta^{*}=-[\frac{1}{2i}\Phi,\eta^{*}]
, and

D^{\prime\prime}\eta^{*}+\beta^{*}=\bar{\partial}_{J}(\eta^{*})=(\partial_{A_{J}}\eta)^{*}=\psi^{*}.

By the above calculations, we have

\mathrm{d}\mathrm{H}_{(J,\Phi)}(\mu,\beta,\psi)=\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\beta+\psi+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu+\beta^{*}-\psi^{*}-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right)=D\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\left(\eta-\eta^{*}\right)\right)~.

By Lemma
5.11
, this implies the vector
w
is in the isomonodromic distribution
\mathcal{D}_{x}
, as desired.
∎

5.4.
The energy function on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})

Recall that we have fixed a structure group reduction
P_{\mathsf{K}}\subset P
to the maximal compact subgroup.
Let
(J,\Phi)
be a stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle which solves the Hitchin
equation for the fixed reduction
P_{K}.
The
L^{2}
-norm of the Higgs field
defines the
energy function

\mathrm{E}:\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathbb{R}
. Explicitly,

(5.6)

\mathrm{E}([J,\Phi])=\|\Phi\|^{2}=-2\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\Psi\wedge\Psi\circ j\right),

where
\Psi=\tfrac{1}{2i}(\Phi-\Phi^{*})
and
j=\pi(J)
is the induced complex structure on
\Sigma.

Definition 5.16
.

Fix a representation
\rho\in\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
. Let
s_{\rho}:\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)\to\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
be the section whose image is the isomonodromic leaf of

\rho.
Then the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}
of
\rho
is defined to be the function

\mathcal{E}_{\rho}=\mathrm{E}\circ s_{\rho}:\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{R}.

Remark 5.17
.

As mentioned in the introduction,
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}([X])
is the energy of
the (unique)
\rho
-equivariant harmonic map
\widetilde{X}\to\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{K}

(cf.
[
26
,
29
]
).

The first and second variation formula for harmonic maps is classical
(see
[
35
, §6]
). Here we give a formulation of this for Higgs bundles (see
also
[
90
]
).

Let
(J_{t},\Phi_{t})
be a family of stable Higgs bundles which solve the Hitchin equations. Denote the tangent vector at
t=0
by
(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{J},\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Phi})
and let
m=\mathrm{d}\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{J})\in\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
. The associated first and second variation of the energy is given by

(5.7)

\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathrm{E}}(J_{t},\Phi_{t})=-2\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(2\Psi\wedge\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\circ j+\Psi\wedge\Psi\circ m\right),

(5.8)

\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathrm{E}}(J_{t},\Phi_{t})=-2\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(2\Psi\wedge\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\circ j+2\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\wedge\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\circ j+4\Psi\wedge\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\circ m+\Psi\wedge\Psi\circ\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{m}\right)~.

The first variation of
\mathrm{E}
in semiharmonic directions is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
be a semiharmonic vector at
(J,\Phi)
, then the first variation of
\mathrm{E}
in the direction
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is given by

\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathrm{E}}(\mu,\beta,\psi)=2\mathrm{Re}\left\langle\Phi,\psi-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right\rangle+2\mathrm{Re}\left\langle\Phi,\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right\rangle=2\mathrm{Re}\left\langle\Phi,\psi\right\rangle.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.24
,
the first variation of the metric in semiharmonic directions vanishes. So,

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 54.50969pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-54.50969pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Phi}=\psi+\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 78.50969pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 124.62083pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}=\frac{1}{2i}\left(\psi+\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu-\psi^{*}-\left(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Since
\psi
and
\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu
have types
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
respectively, we have

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(2\Psi\wedge\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\circ j\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\left(\Phi-\Phi^{*}\right)\wedge\left(i\psi-i\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu+i\psi^{*}-i\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\Phi\wedge\left(\psi^{*}-\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\right)+\frac{i}{2}\int\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\left(\psi-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right)\wedge\Phi^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\left\langle\Phi,\psi-\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right\rangle

Similarly, since
\Psi\circ m=\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu+\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}
we have

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\Psi\wedge\Psi\circ m\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\Phi-\Phi^{*}\right)\wedge\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu+\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\Phi\wedge\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)+\frac{i}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\wedge\Phi^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\left\langle\Phi,\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right\rangle.

Adding the two terms and using (
5.7
) completes the proof.
∎

Along the horizontal and isomonodromic distributions we have the following.

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
x=[J,\Phi]\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
and let
w_{[\mu]}\in\mathcal{H}_{x}
and
\ell_{[\mu]}\in\mathcal{D}_{x}
be horizontal and isomonodromic vectors, respectively. Then the derivative of
\mathrm{E}
in the directions
w_{[\mu]}
and
\ell_{[\mu]}
are

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 29.50642pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.50642pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{E}\left(w_{[\mu]}\right)=0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 53.50642pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 99.35078pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{E}(\ell_{[\mu]})=2\mathrm{Re}\left\langle\Phi,\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right\rangle}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Proof.

Let
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
be a semiharmonic representative of the horizontal vector
w_{[\mu]}
. By Lemma
5.8
,
\psi^{*}=\bar{\partial}_{J}\eta
for some
\eta\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
. By Lemma
5.18
and Stokes’ theorem we have
\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathrm{E}}(\mu,\beta,\psi)=0
.

Similarly, let
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
be a semiharmonic representative b of the isomonodromic vector
\ell_{[\mu]}
. By Lemma
5.12
,
\psi^{*}-(\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)=\bar{\partial}\zeta
for some
\zeta\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
. Hence, by Stokes’ theorem, along the isomonodromic distribution we have

(5.9)

-\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(2\Psi\wedge\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\circ j\right)=0.

Lemma
5.18
now implies
\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathrm{E}}(\mu,\beta,\psi)=2\mathrm{Re}\langle\Phi,\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\rangle
.
∎

Remark 5.20
.

The second equation in Lemma
5.19

agrees with the well-known first variation of the energy
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}
,
the formula for which goes back to Douglas (cf.
[
32
, Equation 12.29]
).

We will also need the second variation of
\mathrm{E}
along the isomonodromic distribution.

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
(J_{t},\Phi_{t})
be a path of Higgs bundles whose tangent vector is an isomonodromic semiharmonic vector
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
. Then the second variation of
\mathrm{E}
is given by

\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathrm{E}}(J_{t},\Phi_{t})=-2\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(2\Psi\wedge\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\Psi}\circ m+\Psi\wedge\Psi\circ\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}\right)~=~8\Real\left\langle\partial_{A_{J}}(i\zeta),\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right\rangle-2\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\Psi\wedge\Psi\circ\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}\right),

where
\zeta\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
is given by Lemma
5.12
.

Proof.

The second variation is given by Equation (
5.8
). By differentiating Equation (
5.9
), along the isomonodromic distribution, we have

0=-\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(2\Psi\wedge\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\circ j+2\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\wedge\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\circ j+2\Psi\wedge\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\circ m\right).

Hence, for a path
(J_{t},\Phi_{t})
which is tangent to the isomonodromic distribution at
t=0
, we have

\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathrm{E}}(J_{t},\Phi_{t})=-2\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(2\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\wedge\Psi\circ m+\Psi\wedge\Psi\circ\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}\right).

To complete the proof, we compute
-\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(2\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\wedge\Psi\circ m\right)
. By Lemma
5.11
and Lemma
5.12
, there is a unique
\zeta\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
such that
\zeta=\zeta^{*}
and
\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}=\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\zeta
. Hence,

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(2\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\Psi}\wedge\Psi\circ m\right)

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\zeta\wedge\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu+\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}(i\zeta)\wedge\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)-2i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\wedge\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}(i\zeta)\right)

\displaystyle=2\langle\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}(i\zeta),\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\rangle+2\left\langle\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*},\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}(i\zeta)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=4\Real\left\langle\partial_{A_{J}}(i\zeta),\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right\rangle,

where we used that
\zeta=\zeta^{*}
implies
\left(\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}(i\zeta)\right)^{*}=-\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}(i\zeta)
.
∎

5.5.
Proof of Theorem
C

Fix a representation
\rho\in\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
. The complex Hessian of the energy function
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}:\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{R}
of
\rho
is defined by
\partial\bar{\partial}\mathcal{E}_{\rho}.
The following is equivalent to Theorem
C
of the introduction.

Theorem 5.22
.

Fix a representation
\rho\in\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
and let
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}:\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{R}
be the energy function of
\rho
. Fix a point
[j]\in\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
and let
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
be the point in the isomonodromic leaf of
\rho
in the fiber over
[j]
.
Then for each tangent vector
[\mu]\in\mathrm{T}_{[j]}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
, the complex Hessian of
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}
at
[j]
along the complex line spanned by
[\mu]
is

\partial_{[\mu]}\partial_{[\overline{\mu}]}\mathcal{E}_{\rho}=-2\|w_{[\mu]}\|_{h_{0}}^{2}=2\|\Theta([\mu])\|^{2},

where
w_{[\mu]}
is the horizontal vector associated
[\mu],
and
\Theta
is the holomorphic section from Theorem
4.6
.

Remark 5.23
.

Note that the complex Hessian of
\mathrm{E}
along the complex line spanned by
the isomonodromic vector
\ell_{\mu}
is different than what is computed
above since the isomonodromic distribution is not complex.

Since
\|\Theta([\mu])\|_{h_{0}}^{2}\geq 0
,
the following corollary is immediate from Theorem
5.15
.

Corollary 5.24
.

Let
\rho\in\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
. Then the energy function
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}
is plurisubharmonic.
Moreover, for
[j]\in\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
, the set of directions
[\mu]
in
which
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}
is not strictly plurisubharmonic is identified with
the intersection of
\ker(\Theta)
and the tangent space to
s_{\rho}([j])
.

Remark 5.25
.

The first statement in Corollary
5.24
was proven by Toledo

[
89
]
, in a more general context, and later also by Tošić

[
90
]
. The formula in Theorem
5.22

above makes plurisubharmonicity manifest.
In Appendix
D
, we show that this formula agrees
with the expression found in
[
90
, Theorem 1.10]
. Additionally, in

[
90
, Theorem 1.6]

it is shown that
\mu
is in the kernel of the complex Hessian of the energy function
if and only there exists a
\xi_{\mu}\in\Omega^{0}(P(\mathfrak{g}))

(5.10)

\textstyle{\bar{\partial}_{J}\xi_{\mu}=\Phi\mu}
and
\textstyle{[\xi_{\mu},\Phi]=0.}

This, too, is clearly equivalent to being in the kernel of
\Theta
.
Indeed, by definition,
\mu\in\ker(\Theta_{(J,\Phi)})
if and only if

\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu
is in image of
D^{\prime\prime}
, which is equivalent to the
existence of such a
\xi_{\mu}.
In this way,
the holomorphicity of the degeneracy locus is also immediate.

Proof.

Let
j(s,t)
be a two parameter family of complex structures on
\Sigma
which is holomorphic in the variable
s+it
and satisfies
j(0,0)=j
. If
m_{s},m_{t}
are first variations of
j(s,t)
at
0
with respect to
s,t
, respectively, then the complex Hessian of
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}
at
j
in complex line
m_{s}+im_{t}
is given

(5.11)

\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\bar{\mu}}E_{\rho}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathcal{E}_{\rho}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}+\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathcal{E}_{\rho}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\right)~.

Let
\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}_{s},\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}_{t}
be the second variations at
0
of
j(s,t)
with respect
s
and
t
, respectively. Then, as in
[
89
, Equation 17]
, differentiating the Cauchy-Riemann equations implies

(5.12)

\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{m}_{s}+\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{m}_{t}=2jm_{s}^{2}.

Hence, the complex Hessian is computed by adding the two second variations from Lemma
5.21
for the isomonodromic vectors
\ell_{[\mu]}
and
\ell_{[i\mu]}
, where the sums of the two
\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{m}
terms satisfy (
5.12
). Furthermore, it is straight forward to check that
2jm^{2}=2|\mu|^{2}j.

Let
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,P(\mathfrak{g}))
be the associated sections from Lemma
5.11
for the semiharmonic isomonodromic vectors
(\mu,\beta_{1},\psi_{1})
and
(i\mu,\beta_{2},\psi_{2})
, respectively. We have

\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{[\mu]}\partial_{[\overline{\mu}]}\mathcal{E}_{\rho}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\Psi\wedge\Psi\circ|\mu|^{2}j\right)+\Real\left\langle\partial_{A_{J}}(i\zeta_{1}),\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\right\rangle+\Real\left\langle\partial_{A_{J}}(i\zeta_{2}),\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi i\mu\right)^{*}\right\rangle.

For the first term we have

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\Psi\wedge\Psi\circ|\mu|^{2}j\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}|\mu|^{2}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(-i\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\wedge\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*}+i\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*}\wedge\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=i\int_{\Sigma}|\mu|^{2}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\wedge\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\right)^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}.

Using Stokes’ theorem and

D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)=\mathrm{d}_{A_{J}}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)
,
we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{[\mu]}\partial_{[\overline{\mu}]}\mathcal{E}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}+\Real\langle\partial_{A_{J}}(i\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2})\wedge\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}+\Real\left\{i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\partial_{A_{J}}(i\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2})\wedge\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}-\Real\left\{i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left((i\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2})\wedge D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\right)\right\}~.

Let
w_{\mu}=(\mu,\beta,\psi)
be a semiharmonic horizontal vector associated to
\mu
. Recall that

D^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)=-D^{\prime\prime}(\beta+\psi)=-\bar{\partial}\psi-[\Phi,\beta]\ ,

and, by invariance of the Killing form, we have

\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left((i\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2})\wedge\left[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi,\beta\right]\right)=\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\left[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi,(i\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2})\right]\wedge\beta\right).

Using
D^{\prime\prime}=\bar{\partial}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi
, Stokes’ theorem and (
5.5
), we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{[\mu]}\partial_{[\overline{\mu}]}\mathcal{E}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}+\Real\left\{i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left((i\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2})\wedge D^{\prime\prime}(\beta+\psi)\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}-\Real\left\{i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(D^{\prime\prime}(i\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2})\wedge(\beta+\psi)\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}-\Real\left\{i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left((-\psi^{*}+\beta^{*})\wedge(\beta+\psi)\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}-\|\beta\|^{2}-\|\psi\|^{2}~.

This completes the proof since
\|w_{\mu}\|^{2}_{h_{0}}=\|\psi\|^{2}+\|\beta\|^{2}-\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}.

∎

6.
Stratification and isomonodromic leaves

We now discuss the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on the joint moduli space
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
, Higgs bundles for real forms of
\mathsf{G}
and the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
and mapping class group invariant stratification of
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
defined by the rank of the kernel of the hermitian form
h_{0}
, or equivalently, the 2-form
\omega_{0}.
We then prove Theorems
6.16
and
6.8
which are equivalent to Theorems D and E from the introduction, respectively.

6.1.
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action

Given a complex manifold
N
, we denote by
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}[N]
the group of nowhere
vanishing holomorphic functions on
N
. When
N=\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, the group

\mathbb{C}^{\ast}[\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)]
acts on the configuration space
\mathcal{C}(P)
via

\Xi:\mathbb{C}^{\ast}[\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)]\times\mathcal{C}(P)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(P)~:~(f,(J,\Phi))\longmapsto(J,f(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(J))\Phi)\ .

This action preserves the space
F^{-1}(0)^{s}
of stable Higgs bundles. If
we denote by
p:\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\to\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
the quotient map, the pullback
gives
