p^{*}:\mathbb{C}^{*}[\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)]\to\mathbb{C}^{*}[\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)]
.
We can thus define the action of an element
f\in\mathbb{C}^{*}[\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)]
on
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
by

f\cdotp x:=\left[\Xi(p^{*}f,x_{0})\right]~,

where
x_{0}\in F^{-1}(0)^{s}
is any lift of
x
.
Restricting the action to constant functions yields a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
that
restricts to the usual
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action of Hitchin on each fiber.

Proposition 6.1
.

The action of
\mathbb{C}^{*}[\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)]
on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
described above is holomorphic.

Proof.

For a Higgs bundle
x=(J,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(P)
and a vector
(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{f},(\mu,\beta,\theta))\in\mathrm{T}_{f}\mathbb{C}^{*}[\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)]\times\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{C}(P)
,
(where canonically,
\mathrm{T}_{f}\mathbb{C}^{*}[\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)]\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)]
)
the differential of
\Xi
is given by

\mathrm{d}_{(f,x)}\Xi(\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{f},(\mu,\beta,\theta))=(\mu,\beta,\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}\Phi+f\theta)

and so
\Xi
is holomorphic.

The pullback map
p^{*}
and the projection
\pi:F^{-1}(0)^{s}\to\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
are both holomorphic.
Around any point
y\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
, one can find a holomorphic slice
\mathcal{S}

in
F^{-1}(0)^{s}
such that the restriction of
\pi
to
\mathcal{S}
is a
biholomorphism onto a neighborhood of
y
. The action of

\mathbb{C}^{*}[\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)]
on
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
is then locally given by the
composition
\pi\circ\Xi\circ\pi|_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
, which is thus holomorphic.
∎

6.1.1.
The horizontal distribution

We now show that the restriction of this action to
\mathsf{U}(1)
preserves the horizontal distribution
\mathcal{H}
from Definition
5.1
.

Proposition 6.2
.

For
\lambda\in\mathsf{U}(1)
denote the multiplication map
m_{\lambda}:\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
. Then, the derivative
\mathrm{d}m_{\lambda}
preserves the horizontal distribution
\mathcal{H}.

Proof.

The map
m_{\lambda}
is induced from the map
m_{\lambda}:\mathcal{C}(P)\to\mathcal{C}(P)
defined by
m_{\lambda}(J,\Phi)=(J,\lambda\Phi)
.
Let
x=(J,\Phi)
be a stable Higgs bundle which solves the Hitchin equations. Then for
\lambda\in\mathsf{U}(1)
,
y=(J,\lambda\Phi)
also solves the Hitchin equations.
It suffices to prove
\mathrm{d}_{x}m_{\lambda}:\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{C}(P)\to\mathrm{T}_{y}\mathcal{C}(P)
sends horizontal semiharmonic vectors at
x
to horizontal semiharmonic vectors at
m_{\lambda}(x).

For a tangent vector
v=(\mu,\beta,\psi)\in\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{C}(P)
, we have
\mathrm{d}_{x}m_{\lambda}(v)=(\mu,\beta,\lambda\psi).

By Lemma
5.8
,
v
is semi-harmonic and horizontal if and only if there exists
\eta\in\Omega^{0}(P[\mathfrak{g}])
such that

\textstyle{D^{\prime}_{x}(\eta)=(\beta,\psi)}
and
\textstyle{D^{\prime\prime}_{x}(\beta,\psi)+D^{\prime}_{x}(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)=0,}

where
D^{\prime}_{x}=\partial_{A_{J}}+(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi)^{*}
and
D^{\prime\prime}_{x}=\overline{\partial}_{A_{J}}+\frac{1}{2i}\Phi
.
Setting

\textstyle{D^{\prime}_{y}=\partial_{A_{J}}+(\tfrac{1}{2i}\lambda\Phi)^{*}}
and
\textstyle{D^{\prime\prime}_{y}=\overline{\partial}_{A_{J}}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\lambda\Phi~,}

we have

\displaystyle\lambda D^{\prime\prime}_{x}(\beta,\psi)+\lambda D^{\prime}_{x}(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)

\displaystyle=\overline{\partial}_{A_{J}}\lambda\psi+\tfrac{1}{2i}[\lambda\Phi,\beta]+\tfrac{1}{2i}\partial_{A_{J}}\lambda\Phi\mu

\displaystyle=D^{\prime\prime}_{y}(\beta,\lambda\psi)+D^{\prime}_{y}(\lambda\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu).

Since
\lambda\in\mathsf{U}(1)
, we have

D_{y}^{\prime}(\lambda\eta)=\partial_{A_{J}}\lambda\eta+[(\tfrac{1}{2i}\lambda\Phi)^{*},\lambda\eta]=\lambda\partial_{A_{J}}\eta+[(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi)^{*},\eta]=\lambda\psi+\beta.

Hence,
(\mu,\beta,\lambda\psi)
is a semiharmonic horizontal vector in
\mathrm{T}_{y}\mathcal{C}(P)
if and only if
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
is a semiharmonic horizontal vector in
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{C}(P)
. This completes the proof.
∎

6.1.2.
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed points and cyclic Higgs bundles

The fixed points of the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on the moduli
\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})
of Higgs bundles for a fixed Riemann surface
X
are important from many perspectives. For example,
Simpson showed that, under the nonabelian Hodge correspondence,
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed points correspond to complex variations of Hodge structure
[
81
,
84
]
.
In addition, Hitchin showed that
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed points are the critical points of the energy function
\mathrm{E}_{X}
on
\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})

[
48
]
. This also holds on the joint moduli space.

Proposition 6.3
.

A point
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
is a critical point of the energy function
\mathrm{E}
from (
5.6
) if and only if it is a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed point.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.19
, the first variation of the energy is always zero in the horizontal distribution.
By the above discussion, the first variation of the energy in the vertical directions vanishes exactly at the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed points. This completes the proof.
∎

In
[
84
]
, Simpson proves that a Higgs bundle is a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed point if and only if it is fixed by an infinite order element of
\mathsf{U}(1)<\mathbb{C}^{*}
. As a result, we denote the set of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed points by

\mathbf{Y}_{\infty}=\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})^{\mathbb{C}^{*}}~.

The set of Higgs bundles fixed by a cyclic subgroup of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
are also of interest.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{k}<\mathbb{C}^{*}
be the subgroup of
k^{th}
-roots of unity. A
k
-cyclic Higgs bundle
is a point in
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
fixed by the action
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
. Denote the set of
k
-cyclic Higgs bundles by
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
.

We have the following proposition concerning the sets of fixed points.

Proposition 6.5
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the set
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
is a holomorphic subbundle of
\pi:\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
such that for any
x
in
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
, the horizontal space
\mathcal{H}_{x}
is contained in
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{k}
.

Proof.

Let
\zeta
be a unit norm complex number generating
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
. So

\mathbf{Y}_{k}=\{x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})~|~\zeta x=x\}

is the set of fixed points of a holomorphic action, hence is a holomorphic submanifold.
Given
x\in\mathbf{Y}_{k}
, we have

\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{k}=\ker(\mathrm{d}m_{\zeta}-\mathrm{Id})~.

By Proposition
6.2
, the horizontal space
\mathcal{H}_{x}
is preserved by
\mathrm{d}m_{\zeta}
. The fact
\mathrm{d}m_{\zeta}
is the identity on
\mathcal{H}_{x}
follows directly from
\pi(\zeta y)=\pi(y)
for any
y\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
. In particular

\mathcal{H}_{x}\subset\ker(m_{\zeta}-\mathrm{Id})=\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{\zeta}~.

Since
\mathrm{d}_{x}\pi
restricts to an isomorphism on
\mathcal{H}_{x}
,
\pi
restricts to a holomorphic submersion on
\mathbf{Y}_{\zeta}
.
∎

Let us now describe a nice application of Proposition
6.5
. The uniformization theorem associates to any point
X\in\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
a Fuchsian representation
\rho_{X}\in\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C})
, and hence a
uniformizing Higgs bundle
defined by

u(X):=\mathrm{H}_{X}^{-1}(\rho_{X})\in\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C})~.

We thus obtain the
uniformizing section

u:\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)\to\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C})
.

Proposition 6.6
.

The uniformizing section is holomorphic and horizontal.

Proof.

For any choice
X\in\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
, the uniformizing Higgs bundle
u(X)
is an isolated
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed point in
\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C})
,
[
47
]
. Hence, the image of the uniformizing section is a connected component of
\mathbf{Y}_{\infty}
, and is thus holomorphic and horizontal by Proposition
6.5
.
∎

6.2.
Stratification

Recall from Theorem
5.15
that the kernel
\mathcal{K}_{x}
of the hermitian form
h_{0}
at a point
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
is identified with the kernel of the holomorphic section
\Theta
at
x
. The dimension of the kernel of
\Theta
defines a stratification of the moduli space
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
which is preserved by the actions both
\mathbb{C}^{*}
and the mapping class group of
\Sigma
.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let

\mathbf{M}_{d}=\{x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})~|~\dim(\mathcal{K}_{x})=d\}~.

Then

(1)

\mathbf{M}_{d}
is a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-invariant complex subvariety of
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
which is mapping class group invariant and empty if
d>3g-3
,

(2)

the closures of the subsets are nested, i.e., they satisfy

\overline{\mathbf{M}}_{d}\subset\coprod_{d\leq c}\mathbf{M}_{c},

(3)

\mathbf{M}_{0}
is nonempty, open and dense, and

(4)

\mathbf{M}_{3g-3}
is nonempty and closed.

In particular,
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})=\coprod\limits_{0\leq d\leq 3g-3}\mathbf{M}_{d}
is a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-invariant stratification.

Proof.

The proof mainly follows from the identification between the kernel of the hermitian form
h_{0}
the kernel of the holomorphic section
\Theta
from Theorem
B
(see Theorem
5.15
).
The points that do not immediately follow from Theorem
B
are the nonemptiness of
\mathbf{M}_{0}
and
\mathbf{M}_{3g-3}.

For the nonemptiness of
\mathbf{M}_{0}
, the points in the so called Hitchin section are always in
\mathbf{M}_{0}
, see Example
6.13
below. For nonemptiness of
\mathbf{M}_{3g-3}
, note that
x\in\mathbf{M}_{3g-3}
for all points
x
where the Higgs field
\Phi
is zero since the map
\Theta_{x}
is identically zero.
∎

Recall that Corollaries
1.5
and
1.6
give equivalent characterizations of the open and closed strata, respectively, in terms of the isomonodromic distribution. They both follow directly from Theorems
5.15
,
5.22
and
6.7
.

The following is Theorem
E
from the introduction.

Theorem 6.8
.

For a representation
\rho\in\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G}),
the following are equivalent:

(1)

The isomonodromic leaf
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
is contained in the closed stratum
\mathbf{M}_{3g-3}
.

(2)

The isomonodromic leaf
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
is a holomorphic submanifold of
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
.

(3)

The energy function
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}
is constant.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.15
, the isomonodromic leaf
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
is contained in the closed stratum if and only if its tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{L}_{\rho}=\mathcal{D}_{x}
is a complex subspace of
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
. So (1) and (2) are equivalent.
If the energy function
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}
is constant, then, at every
x\in\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
, its complex Hessian is zero at every point. So, (3) implies (1) by Theorem
5.22
.
Finally, if
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}\subset\mathbf{M}_{3g-3}
, then the first variation of the energy function
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}
is zero for all
x\in\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
by Lemma
5.19
, so (1) implies (3).
∎

The following corollary is immediate from Proposition
6.3
.

Corollary 6.9
.

If an isomonodromic leaf is fixed pointwise by the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action then it is holomorphic.

6.3.
Points in
\mathbf{M}_{0}
and Proof of Theorem
D

We start with the following sufficient condition for a Higgs bundle
[J,\Phi]
to be in the open stratum
\mathbf{M}_{0}
.

Proposition 6.10
.

Suppose
\mathsf{H}<\mathsf{G}
is a complex reductive subgroup such that the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
has an
\mathsf{H}
-invariant splitting
\mathfrak{g}=U_{1}\oplus U_{2}
with
\dim(U_{1})=1
. Let
(J,\Phi)
be a stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle such that
(P,J)
admits a holomorphic reduction
P_{\mathsf{H}}
to
\mathsf{H}
, and write

\Phi=(\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})\in H^{0}(P_{\mathsf{H}}(U_{1})\otimes K)\oplus H^{0}(P_{\mathsf{H}}(U_{2})\otimes K)\cong H^{0}(P(\mathfrak{g})\otimes K).

If
\Phi_{1}
is nowhere vanishing, then
x=[J,\Phi]
is in the open stratum
\mathbf{M}_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
[J,\Phi]
be a stable
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle and let
P_{\mathsf{H}}\subset P
be
a holomorphic reduction to
\mathsf{H}
, as in the statement. Consider
\mu
a Beltrami differential and a
\xi\in\Omega^{0}(P(\mathfrak{g}))
such that
\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu=\bar{\partial}_{J}\xi
. Since the reduction
P_{\mathsf{H}}\subset P
is holomorphic, we have
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
for
\xi_{j}\in\Omega^{0}(P_{\mathsf{H}}(U_{j}))
and

\bar{\partial}_{J}\xi=(\bar{\partial}_{J}\xi_{1},\bar{\partial}_{J}\xi_{2})=(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi_{1}\mu,\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi_{2}\mu).

Assume that
\Phi_{1}
is a nowhere vanishing section of the line bundle
P_{\mathsf{H}}(U_{1})\otimes K
, hence

\bar{\partial}_{j}(2i\xi_{1}\otimes\Phi_{1}^{-1})=\mu.

It follows that
\mu
defines a zero cohomology class, that is
[\mu]=0\in\mathrm{T}_{[j]}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
. So
[J,\Phi]\in\mathbf{M}_{0}
.
∎

6.3.1.
Examples of points in the open stratum

There are many examples of Higgs bundles that satisfy the assumptions of
Proposition
6.10
.
Below we describe two families of interest.

Example 6.11
.

An
\mathsf{SL}_{n}\mathbb{C}
-Higgs bundle
(J,\Phi)

can be viewed equivalently as a pair
(E,\Phi)
, where
E
is a rank

n
holomorphic vector bundle with fixed trivial determinant, and

\Phi:E\to E\otimes K
is a traceless holomorphic bundle map.
Suppose
E
decomposes holomorphically as

E=E_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{\ell}.

With respect to this decomposition a Higgs field
\Phi

decomposes as
\Phi_{ab}:E_{a}\to E_{b}\otimes K
.
If
E_{a}\cong E_{b}\otimes K
for some
a,b
and
\Phi_{ab}
is an
isomorphism, then the Higgs bundle
(E,\Phi)
satisfies the assumptions
of Proposition
6.10
.
To see this, it suffices to consider the case when
E=E_{1}\oplus E_{2}
with
E_{2}=E_{1}\otimes K^{-1}
and
\Phi_{12}=\mathrm{Id}
. In this situation the structure group of the bundle reduces to

\mathsf{H}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&\lambda A\end{pmatrix}\in\mathsf{SL}_{2k}\mathbb{C}~|~A\in\mathsf{GL}_{k}\mathbb{C}\text{ and }\lambda=\det(A)^{-2}\right\}

This subgroup preserves the splitting
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}\mathbb{C}=U_{1}\oplus U_{2}
, where

U_{1}=\left\langle\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\mathrm{Id}&0\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle\ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ U_{2}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{pmatrix}~|~\Tr(Z)=0\right\}.

Remark 6.12
.

Let
(E,\Phi)
be a stable
\mathsf{SL}_{n}\mathbb{C}
-Higgs bundle which is
k
-cyclic and not a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed point, i.e., a point in
\mathbf{Y}_{k}\setminus\mathbf{Y}_{\infty}.

By a result of Simpson
[
80
]
,

E
decomposes as

E=E_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{k},

and, with respect to this decomposition, the components of the Higgs field
\Phi_{ab}:E_{a}\to E_{b}\otimes K
are zero when
b-a\neq 1\mod~k.

In particular, if
(E,\Phi)\in\mathbf{Y}_{k}\setminus\mathbf{Y}_{\infty}
, and one of the nonzero maps

\Phi_{ab}:E_{a}\xrightarrow{\hskip 11.38092pt\cong\hskip 8.19447pt}E_{b}\otimes K

is an isomorphism, then
(E,\Phi)
is in the open stratum
\mathbf{M}_{0}.

See §
7.3
for many examples of Higgs bundles that are
special cases of Example
6.11
and which have been studied in the literature.

The second class of examples come from the so called Slodowy slice construction for an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\mathbb{C}
subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}.
The Higgs bundle analogue below generalizes Hitchin’s description
[
48
]
of the Hitchin section, and was introduced in
[
22
]
.

Example 6.13
.

Suppose
\{f,h,e\}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is a subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, where

\textstyle{[h,e]=2e,}
\textstyle{[h,f]=-2f,}
and
\textstyle{[e,f]=h.}

Let
\mathsf{T}<\mathsf{G}
be the subgroup defined by exponentiating
\langle h\rangle
and
\mathsf{C}<\mathsf{G}
be the centralizer of
\{f,h,e\}
. Finally, let
\mathsf{H}<\mathsf{G}
be the subgroup generated by
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{T}.
Then
\mathfrak{g}
admits a
\mathsf{H}
-invariant splitting

\mathfrak{g}=W\oplus\langle f\rangle\oplus\bigoplus_{j=0}^{N}V_{j},

where
V_{j}=\{v\in\mathfrak{g}~|~[e,v]=0\text{ and }[h,v]=jv\}
. The (Lie algebra) Slodowy slice of
\{f,h,e\}
is

\mathfrak{s}_{f}=\{f+v_{0}+\cdots+v_{N}|~v_{j}\in V_{j}\}\subset\mathfrak{g}.

We now recall a Higgs bundle analogue.
Since
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{C}
are commuting subgroups, the multiplication map
\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{T}\to\mathsf{G}
is a group homomorphism.
Let
P_{\mathsf{T}}
and
P_{\mathsf{C}}
be holomorphic principal
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{C}
bundles, respectively, and
P_{\mathsf{H}}
be the holomorphic principal
\mathsf{H}
obtained by extending the structure group with the multiplication map.
If
P_{\mathsf{T}}
is the holomorphic frame bundle of a square root of
K
, then
P_{\mathsf{H}}(\langle f\rangle)\cong K^{-1}
. In particular,
f
defines a section

f\in H^{0}\left(P_{\mathsf{H}}\left(\langle f\rangle\right)\otimes K\right).

The
(Higgs bundle) Slodowy slice
of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple
\{f,h,e\}
is given by

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{f}=\{(P,\Phi)=(P_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{G}),f+\psi_{0}+\cdots\psi_{N})~|~\psi_{j}\in H^{0}(P_{\mathsf{H}}(V_{j})\otimes K)\}~.

The subset of such Higgs bundles which are stable satisfy the
assumptions of Proposition
6.10
, and so define a mapping class group invariant subset of the open stratum
\mathbf{M}_{0}
.
For principal
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\mathbb{C}
-triples, the components of the Higgs bundle Slodowy slice are called Hitchin sections. All Higgs bundles in the Hitchin sections are stable
[
48
]
.

6.3.2.
Proof of Theorem
D

Fix an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\mathbb{C}
-triple
\{f,h,e\}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{f}(X)\subset\mathbf{M}_{0}
denote the Higgs bundle in the Slodowy slice from Example
6.13
on a fixed Riemann surface
X
which are stable.
Furthermore, define
\mathcal{S}_{f}^{0}(X)\subset\mathcal{S}_{f}(X)
to be the subset where the holomorphic section
\psi_{0}
vanishes

\mathcal{S}_{f}^{0}(X)=\{[J,\Phi]\in\mathcal{S}_{f}(X)~|~\psi_{0}=0\}.

Applying the nonabelian Hodge map (
4.10
), defines subsets of the character variety

\mathrm{H}(\mathcal{S}_{f}^{0}(X))\subset\mathrm{H}(\mathcal{S}_{f}(X))\subset\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G}).

In general, it is not clear how these subsets depend on the choice Riemann surface
X
.
However, for the special class of
magical
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triples
introduced in
[
15
]
, we have the following.

Theorem 6.14

(
[
15
]
)
.

Let
\{f,h,e\}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be a magical
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple and
X\in\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
be a Riemann surface. Then
\mathrm{H}(\mathcal{S}_{f}^{0}(X))\subset\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
is independent of
X.

More precisely, for each magical
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple
\{f,h,e\}
it was shown that there is a canonical real form
\mathsf{G}^{\mathbb{R}}_{f}<\mathsf{G}
such that the polystable points of
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{f}^{0}(X)
define a union of connected components of the
\mathsf{G}^{\mathbb{R}}_{f}
-Higgs moduli space on
X
.
In
[
15
]
, the connected components of the
\mathsf{G}^{\mathbb{R}}_{f}
-Higgs bundle moduli space defined by the polystable points of
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{f}^{0}(X)
(and their image in the
\mathsf{G}^{\mathbb{R}}
-character variety) are called
Cayley components
. As a result, we make the following definition.

Definition 6.15
.

We will say that a representation
\rho\in\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
lies in a Cayley component if there is a magical
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple
\{f,h,e\}
such that
\rho\in\mathrm{H}(\mathcal{S}_{f}^{0}(X))
for some (and hence any) Riemann surface
X.

Recall that
\omega_{0}
defines a symplectic form on the open stratum
\mathbf{M}_{0}
which is compatible with
\mathrm{I}
, and that the hermitian form
h_{0}
is nondegenerate with signature
(\dim\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G}),3g-3)
.
Writing
h_{0}=2(g_{0}+i\omega_{0})
,
g_{0}
is a pseudo-Kähler metric on
\mathbf{M}_{0}
.
The following theorem is equivalent to Theorem
D
from the introduction.

Theorem 6.16
.

Let
\rho\in\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
be in a Cayley component and
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}\subset\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
be its isomonodromic leaf. Then, with the notation above, we have

(1)

\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
is contained in the open stratum
\mathbf{M}_{0},

(2)

\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
is a
\omega_{0}
-symplectic submanifold
\mathbf{M}_{0}
which is totally real and
g_{0}
-isotropic, and

(3)

h_{0}
has signature
(3g-3,3g-3)
on
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{L}_{\rho}\oplus\mathrm{I}(\mathrm{T}\mathcal{L}_{\rho})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.14
,
\rho
being in a Cayley component implies
\rho\in\mathrm{H}(\mathcal{S}_{f}^{0}(X))
for every
X\in\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
.
Since,
\mathcal{S}_{f}^{0}(X)\subset\mathbf{M}_{0}
for all
X\in\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
, the isomonodromic leaf
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
is contained in
\mathcal{M}_{0}.

Items (2) and (3) now follow from the definition of
\mathbf{M}_{0}
and Lemma
5.5
.
∎

7.
Hitchin map, minimal surfaces and cyclic locus

In this section, we construct the Hitchin map in the joint setting and give a different interpretation of the section
\Theta
. We then focus on
the moduli spaces of equivariant minimal surfaces and cyclic Higgs bundles, and prove Theorems

F
and
G
and
H
.

7.1.
The Hitchin map

7.1.1.
The bundle of holomorphic
k
-differentials

The bundle of holomorphic
k
-differentials over Teichmüller space is
defined by taking direct images of the
k
-th power of the relative
cotangent sheaf of the universal curve. A direct construction of this
bundle from the point of view of the Ahlfors-Bers complex structure is
given, for example, by Bers
[
9
]
. In this section, we briefly describe a construction
in manner of the previous sections. The result is the following.

Theorem 7.1
.

Fix
k\geq 1
. There is a complex manifold
{\bf B}^{(k)}
and
holomorphic submersion
\pi_{\bf B}:{\bf B}^{(k)}\to{\bf T}(\Sigma)

such that the points of the fiber
{\bf B}_{X}^{(k)}=\pi_{\bf B}^{-1}(X)

consist precisely of the holomorphic
k
-differentials on
X
.
Moreover, there is an action of
{\rm Mod}(\Sigma)
on
{\bf B}^{(k)}

by biholomorphisms for which

\pi_{\bf B}
is equivariant.

Since many of the technical details follow very closely the discussion in
§
4
, in the following we shall only give the set-up for the
construction and omit explicit proofs.

Given
j\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, let
j^{(k)}\in\End((T^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma)^{\otimes k})
be defined by:

j^{(k)}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{k})=\sum_{p=1}^{k}\alpha_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes(\alpha_{p}\circ j)\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{k}.

Then
j^{(k)}
preserves
\Sym^{k}(T^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma)\subset\End((T^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma)^{\otimes k})
, and there is an eigenspace decomposition

\Sym^{k}(T^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma)=\bigoplus_{p=0}^{k}E^{(k)}_{-k+2p}[j],

where
j^{(k)}
acts by
ip
on
E_{p}^{(k)}[j]
. Let

{\bf E}^{(k)}_{p}:=\left\{(j,\alpha)\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\times\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,\Sym^{k}(T^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma))\mid\alpha\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,E_{p}^{(k)}[j])\right\}.

This is clearly the total space of a
C^{\infty}
bundle on
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)

with the Fréchet
topology. Moreover, it carries a natural complex structure.
We call
{\bf E}^{(k)}_{k}
the
bundle of
k
-differentials
on
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
.

Given a smooth family
(j(t))_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
in
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
tangent to
\mu\in\mathrm{T}_{j}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, and
\ z_{t}
is a local holomorphic 1-form on
(\Sigma,j(t))
, then
\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\wideparen{dz^{k}}}=kdz^{k-1}\mu(\partial_{z})
. In particular

\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\wideparen{j^{(k)}}}:E^{(k)}_{k}[j]\longrightarrow E^{(k)}_{k-2}[j]~.

As a result, if
(j(t),q(t))_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
is a smooth family in
{\bf E}^{(k)}_{k}
, then
\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{q}=(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{q})_{k}+(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{q})_{k-2}
, where
(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{q})_{k}
is a
k
-differential, and
(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{q})_{k-2}
is a

(k-2)
-differential given by:

(\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{q})_{k-2}=-\tfrac{i}{2}\overset{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\wideparen{j^{(k)}}}q~.

The holomorphicity condition is given by the zeros of the map:

F:{\bf E}^{(k)}_{k}\longrightarrow{\bf E}^{(k+1)}_{k}:q_{k}\longmapsto\bar{\partial}_{j}q_{k}

induced by the exterior derivative on forms, and
where we regard

\Lambda^{2}T^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma\simeq T^{1,0}\Sigma\otimes T^{0,1}\Sigma\subset\Sym^{2}(T^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma)\ .

Proposition 7.2
.

The zero set
Z^{(k)}
of
F
is a smooth submanifold
of
{\bf E}^{(k)}_{k}
, and
{\rm Diff}_{0}(\Sigma)
acts properly
discontinuously on
Z^{(k)}
.

We then set:
{\bf B}^{(k)}=Z^{(k)}/{\rm Diff}_{0}(\Sigma)
.
The structure of a complex manifold then follows along the lines of §
4
.

7.1.2.
The Hitchin map

Let us first briefly recall the definition of the Hitchin map on
\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})
.
For a fixed Riemann surface
X
, let
{\bf B}^{(k)}_{X}=H^{0}(X,K_{X}^{\otimes k})
.
Given a semisimple complex Lie group
\mathsf{G}
of rank
\ell
, the

Hitchin base
is defined by

(7.1)

{\bf B}_{X}(\mathsf{G})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\ell}{\bf B}^{(m_{i}+1)}_{X}

where
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}
are the exponents of
\mathsf{G}
.
For each exponent
m_{i}
of
\mathsf{G}
, let

p_{i}
denote a choice of nonzero invariant polynomial on
\mathfrak{g}
of
degree
m_{i}+1
. The
Hitchin map
is then

\varpi_{X}:{\bf M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})\longrightarrow{\bf B}_{X}(\mathsf{G}):[\mathcal{E},\Phi]\mapsto(p_{1}(\Phi),\ldots,p_{\ell}(\Phi))~.

Now
let
{\bf B}(\mathsf{G})
denote the fiber product over

{\bf T}(\Sigma)
of the spaces
{\bf B}^{(m_{i}+1)}
,

i=1,\ldots,\ell
, from the previous section.
Let us continue to denote the projection by
\pi_{\bf B}
.
Then
\pi_{\bf B}:{\bf B}(\mathsf{G})\to{\bf T}(\Sigma)
is called the
joint Hitchin
base
.

We can then define:

(7.2)

\varpi([J,\Phi])=[(j,p_{1}(\Phi),\ldots,p_{\ell}(\Phi))]

Using the holomorphicity condition on
\Phi
, the map
\varpi
takes value in
{\bf B}(\mathsf{G})
and is holomorphic since the
p_{\alpha}
are. This is the desired relative Hitchin map.
We summarize with the following.

Theorem 7.3
.

There is a holomorphic map
\varpi
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf M}(\mathsf{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf B}(\mathsf{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf T}(\Sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{{\bf B}}}

commute. Moreover, for every
X\in{\bf T}(\Sigma)
, the restriction of

\varpi
to
\pi^{-1}(X)
is
the Hitchin map
\varpi_{X}
.

7.1.3.
The quadratic part of
\varpi

Note that for any semisimple
\mathsf{G}
, we have
m_{1}=1
. In particular, the
Hitchin map has a quadratic piece
\varpi^{(2)}:\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\to{\bf B}^{(2)}
. This quadratic part is closely related to
\Theta
as we now
explain
2
2

2

We thank Nigel Hitchin for pointing out this interpretation.
(see also
[
20
]
). Here we normalize
\varpi^{(2)}
by

\varpi^{(2)}([J,\Phi])=\left(\pi(J),\tfrac{1}{4}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi\otimes\Phi)\right)~.

Using the identification between
{\bf B}_{X}^{(2)}
and
\mathrm{T}_{X}^{*}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
, any
\mu\in\mathrm{T}_{X}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
defines a function
f_{\mu}
on
\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})
by

f_{\mu}([J,\Phi])=\left\langle\varpi^{(2)}_{X}([J,\Phi]),\mu\right\rangle~.

Here
\langle\cdotp,\cdotp\rangle
is the natural pairing between
H^{0}(K^{2}_{X})
and
H^{1}(X,\mathrm{T}X)
given by

\langle q_{2},\mu\rangle=\int_{\Sigma}q_{2}\mu~,

where
q_{2}\mu
is seen as a
(1,1)
-form on
\Sigma
.

Recall that
\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})
is equipped with a holomorphic symplectic form
\omega_{I}^{\mathbb{C}}
defined by

\omega_{I}^{\mathbb{C}}\left((\beta_{1},\psi_{1}),(\beta_{2},\psi_{2})\right)=i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\psi_{2}\wedge\beta_{1}-\psi_{1}\wedge\beta_{2})~.

Proposition 7.4
.

For any
X\in\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
and
[\mu]\in\mathrm{T}_{X}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
, the vector
field
\Theta([\mu])
on
\mathbf{M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})
is the Hamiltonian vector field
of
f_{\mu}
with respect to
\omega_{I}^{\mathbb{C}}
. In particular, for any
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
we have

\dim\coker\mathrm{d}_{x}\varpi^{(2)}=\dim\ker\Theta_{x}.

Proof.

For
x=[J,\Phi]\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
we have

\mathrm{d}_{x}\varpi_{X}^{(2)}(\beta,\psi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi\otimes\psi)~.

Thus

\mathrm{d}_{x}f_{\mu}(\beta,\psi)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi\otimes\psi)\mu\\
=i\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\psi\wedge\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\right)\\
=4\omega_{I}^{\mathbb{C}}\left(\Theta(\mu),(\beta,\psi)\right)~.

This proves the first statement. To show the second statement, first observe that, evaluating
\varpi^{(2)}
along horizontal vectors, one obtains
\coker\mathrm{d}_{x}\varpi^{(2)}=\coker\mathrm{d}\varpi_{X}^{(2)}
. The result follows from the duality between
\mathrm{d}_{x}f_{\mu}
and
\Theta_{x}(\mu)
.
∎

7.1.4.
The nilpotent cone

We shall not discuss the rich geometry of the Hitchin map, other than to point to
an essential consequence.
For a fixed
X\in\mathrm{T}(\Sigma)
, the
nilpotent cone
\mathbf{C}_{X}(\mathsf{G})
is the preimage of

0\in\mathbf{B}_{X}(\mathsf{G})
by the Hitchin map
\varpi_{X}
. In the joint setting, we define

the (relative) nilpotent cone
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{G})
as the preimage by
\varpi
of the
zero section in
\mathbf{B}(\mathsf{G})
.
We record the following:

Proposition 7.5
.

The nilpotent cone
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{G})
is a complex analytic subvariety of

{\bf M}(\mathsf{G})
.

Recall the definition of the set
\mathcal{U}_{s}
from Theorem

4.6
.

Theorem 7.6
.

There is
s_{0}\geq 0
such that for each
s>s_{0}
, the set
\mathcal{U}_{s}
is an open
neighborhood of
{\bf C}(\mathsf{G})
.

Now let
\vec{q}\in{\bf B}_{X}(\mathsf{G})
. Then
\vec{q}=(q_{k_{1}},\ldots,q_{k_{\ell}})
,
where
k_{i}=m_{i}+1
and
q_{k_{i}}
is a
holomorphic
k_{i}
-differential on
X
.
Define the pointwise norm

|\vec{q}|_{\rho}^{2}(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}|q_{k_{i}}|_{\rho}^{2}(z)

where
\rho
is the hyperbolic metric on
X
(see Appendix

A
).
We set

\|\vec{q}\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{z\in X}|\vec{q}|_{\rho}^{2}(z)

Finally, note that given a Cartan involution
\tau

we can define a pointwise norm for the Higgs field

|\Phi|_{\rho,\tau}(z)
as in Appendix
A
. With this
understood, we have the following.

Lemma 7.7
.

Fix
c>0
. Then there is
K(c)>0
with the following
significance. If
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
is a solution to the Hitchin equations
on a Riemann surface
X
with harmonic metric
\tau
, and

\|\varpi_{X}(J,\Phi)\|_{\infty}\leq c
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in X}|\Phi|_{\rho,\tau}(z)\leq 4K(c)
.

The key point is that
K(c)
is uniform independent of
X
.
Lemma
7.7
is due to Simpson
[
82
, Lemma 2.7]

(see also the proof of
[
94
, Proposition 4.2.21]
).

Proof of Theorem
7.6
.

For
c>0
, it is clear that

{\bf M}^{c}(\mathsf{G})=\left\{[(J,\Phi)]\in{\bf M}(\mathsf{G})\mid\|\varpi_{X}(J,\Phi)\|_{\infty}<c\right\}

is an open neighborhood of the nilpotent cone. If we take

s_{0}=\inf_{c>0}K(c)
, then for any
s>s_{0}
, there is
c>0
such that

K(c)<s
, and so

{\bf M}^{c}(\mathsf{G})\subset\mathcal{U}_{s}
. This completes the proof.
∎

7.2.
Equivariant minimal surfaces

Any point
x=(J,\Phi)
in
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
corresponds to an equivariant harmonic map
u_{x}

from the universal cover of
X=\pi(x)
to the symmetric space of
\mathsf{G}
. The
following is classical.

Proposition 7.8
.

For any point
x
in
\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
, the holomorphic quadratic differential
\varpi^{(2)}(x)
is
proportional to the Hopf differential of the associated equivariant
harmonic map.

Recall that
u_{x}
is a
branched minimal immersion
if and only if its Hopf differential vanishes
(cf.
[
46
,
75
]
.
As a result, the set

\mathbf{W}(\mathsf{G})=\{(J,\Phi)\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\mid p_{1}(\Phi)=0\}

is
the space of equivariant minimal immersions
.

Proposition 7.9
.

The set
\mathbf{W}(\mathsf{G})
is a complex analytic subvariety of
\pi:\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})\to\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
.
The restriction
{\bf W}_{0}:={\bf W}(\mathsf{G})\cap{\bf M}_{0}
is a smooth complex
manifold.

Proof.

If
Z
denotes the image of the zero section in
\mathrm{T}^{*}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma)
, then
\mathbf{W}(\mathsf{G})=(\varpi^{(2)})^{-1}(Z)
and the first statement follows by holomorphicity of
\varpi^{(2)}
.

For the second statement, since points in
\mathbf{M}_{0}
correspond to points on
which the energy is strictly plurisubharmonic (by Corollary
1.5
) and that the complex Hessian of the energy is related to the norm of
\Theta
by Theorem
5.22
, we get that
\Theta
(seen as a section of
\Hom(\pi^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{T}(\Sigma),\mathrm{V}\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})))
is injective at every point of
\mathbf{M}_{0}
. The equality between
\dim\coker\mathrm{d}_{x}\varpi^{(2)}
and
\dim\ker\Theta
of Proposition
7.4
then implies that
\varpi^{(2)}
is a submersion on
\mathbf{M}_{0}
. The result follows.
∎

We now prove Theorem
F
of the introduction.

Theorem 7.10
.

The restriction of
\omega_{0}
to
\mathbf{W}_{0}
is equal to the pullback of the Atiyah-Bott-Goldman symplectic form via (the restriction of) the nonabelian Hodge map.

Proof.

Since

\mathbf{W}_{0}=\left\{(J,\Phi)\in\mathbf{M}_{0}~|~\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi\otimes\Phi)=0\right\}
,
for
x=(J,\Phi)\in\mathbf{W}(\mathsf{G})
, we have

\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}=\big\{(\mu,\theta,\beta)\in\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{M}_{0}~|~\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi\otimes\theta)=0\big\}~.

Since
\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi\otimes\Phi)=0
, we have for any
(\mu,\theta,\beta)\in\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{M}_{0}
that

\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi\otimes\Phi\mu)=0
. Writing

\theta=\psi+\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu
with
\psi
of type

(1,0)
, we get that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi\otimes\psi)=0
. In particular, for any Beltrami differential
\nu
on
X=\pi(x)
, we get we have
\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\psi\wedge\Phi\nu)=0
(this can be easily seen in local coordinates).

From equation (
3.6
), the
(2,0)
-part of
\mathrm{H}^{*}\omega_{ABG}
is given by

\left(\mathrm{H}^{*}\omega_{ABG}\right)^{2,0}_{x}\big((\mu,\theta_{1},\beta_{1}),(\mu,\theta_{2},\beta_{2})\big)=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})=-\frac{1}{8i}\int_{\Sigma}\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\psi_{1}\wedge\Phi\mu_{2}-\psi_{2}\wedge\Phi\mu_{1})~,

and so vanishes on
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}
. The result follows.
∎

Since
\mathbf{M}_{0}
is pseudo-Kähler and not Kähler, the restriction of
h_{0}
to
\mathbf{W}_{0}
is not necessarily nondegenerate (equivalently, the restriction of
\mathrm{H}
to
\mathbf{W}_{0}
is not necessarily a local symplectomorphism). Nevertheless, we can prove the result on the cyclic locus
\mathbf{Y}_{k}\cap\mathbf{W}_{0}
for
k>2
. This is the content of Theorem
G
of the introduction.

Theorem 7.11
.

Let
k>2
and
x
be a point in the
k
-cyclic locus
\mathbf{Y}_{k}\cap\mathbf{W}_{0}
of
\mathbf{W}_{0}
. Then the restriction of the nonabelian Hodge map
\mathrm{H}
to
\mathbf{W}_{0}
is a local symplectomorphism around
x
. In particular,
h_{0}
restricts to a pseudo-Kähler structure on a neighborhood of
x
in
\mathbf{W}_{0}
.

Remark 7.12
.

Recall from Example
6.13
, that the points of any Slodowy slice
\mathcal{S}_{f}
lie in
\mathbf{M}_{0}.

Hence, Theorem
7.11
applies to all
k
-cyclic Higgs bundle, with
k\geq 3,
which lie in some Slodowy slice. For example, all
k
-cyclic Higgs bundles in Hitchin components for
k\geq 3.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{k}
and
\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}
the intersection of the vertical space
\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{M}
with
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{k}
and
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}
respectively. The restriction of
h_{0}
to
\mathbf{W}_{0}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
yields orthogonal decompositions

\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}=\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}\oplus\left(\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}\right)^{\perp}~\ \text{ and }~\ \mathrm{T}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}=\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{k}\oplus\left(\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{k}\right)^{\perp}~.

The joint Hitchin map
\varpi
is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant, where
\mathbb{C}^{*}
acts with weight
k
on the bundle
{\bf B}^{(k)}
of holomorphic
k
-differential. In particular, if
\alpha
is not a multiple of
k
, then
\varpi^{(\alpha)}(x)=0
for any
x\in\mathbf{Y}_{k}
. If follows that
\mathbf{Y}_{k}\cap\mathbf{M}_{0}
is a submanifold of
\mathbf{W}_{0}
, we get that
\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{k}\subset\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}
and so since the dimension coincide, we get
\left(\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}\right)^{\perp}=\left(\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{k}\right)^{\perp}
. Proposition
6.5
the implies

\mathcal{H}_{x}=\left(\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{M}_{0}\right)^{\perp}=\left(\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{W}_{0}\right)^{\perp}=\left(\mathrm{V}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{k}\right)^{\perp}~.

But by definition,
x\in\mathbf{M}_{0}
if and only if
h_{0}
is non-degenerate on
\mathcal{H}_{x}
. The result follows.
∎

7.3.
Cyclic Higgs bundles

We now describe many examples of cyclic Higgs bundles that appeared in the literature.
One can show that each of these examples are in some Slodowy slice for the relevant complex group.
As a result, they are all in
\mathbf{W}_{0}
, see Remark
7.12
(alternatively, since these examples are described in terms of holomorphic vector bundles, and one can also apply Proposition
6.10
and Remark
6.12
). Theorem
7.11
then gives the following result.

Theorem 7.13
.

Let
\mathbf{Z}
be one of the complex submanifolds of
\mathbf{Y}_{k}\cap\mathbf{M}_{0}
described in the following list. Then the nonabelian Hodge map defines a symplectic immersion to the character variety. In particular, real part of
h_{0}
restricts to a nondegenerate pseudo-Kähler metric on
\mathbf{Z}
.

(1)

Hitchin cyclic.
The first extensive study of cyclic Higgs bundles was carried out by Baraglia in his thesis
[
8
]
, and followed by Labourie in
[
55
]
. These works focused on the
k
-cyclic Higgs bundles associated to Hitchin components of the split real form of
\mathsf{G},
when
k-1
is the length of the longest root in
\mathfrak{g}
. In this case, Theorem
7.13
recovers Labourie’s result that
\mathrm{H}
restricts to an immersion
[
55
, Theorem 1.5.1]
.

(2)

Maximal representations in rank
2
.
This second example was considered by Tholozan and the first two authors in
[
23
]
. The
4
-cyclic
\mathsf{SO}_{n+2}\mathbb{C}
-Higgs bundles which give rise to maximal representation into
\mathsf{SO}_{2,n}
.
Such cyclic Higgs bundles are a Gauss lift of maximal surfaces in the pseudo-hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{2,n-1}
. By Theorem
7.13
, the corresponding component
\mathbf{Z}\subset\mathbf{W}(\mathsf{G})
is locally symplectomorphic to the components of maximal representations in
\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
.

(3)

Alternating holomorphic curves in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
.
In
[
24
]
, the first two authors described a one-to-one correspondence between some
6
-cyclic Higgs bundles for the exceptional Lie group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
and some alternate holomorphic curves into the almost-complex pseudo-hyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
. The corresponding Higgs bundles satisfy the condition of Proposition
6.10
, hence Theorem
7.13
provides a stronger result than
[
24
, Theorem C]
.

(4)

A-surfaces in
\bf H^{n,n-1}
.
In
[
69
]
, Nie describes a correspondence between a certain classes of
2n
-cyclic Higgs bundles for
\mathsf{SO}_{n,n+1}
and alternating maximal surfaces in
\mathbf{H}^{n,n}
or
\mathbf{H}^{n-1,n+1}
, depending on the parity of
n
. These Higgs bundles again satisfy the hypothesis of Proposition
6.10
, and so Theorem
7.13
recovers Nie’s infinitesimal rigidity
[
69
, Theorem C]
.

(5)

Non-maximal in
\mathsf{SO}_{2,3}
.
A special class of
4
-cyclic Higgs bundles for
\mathsf{SO}_{2,3}
was considered in
[
23
]
in relation to geometric structures. These Higgs bundles were shown to define Anosov representations by Filip
[
39
]
and Zhang
[
98
]
. These results can also be generalized to
\mathsf{SO}_{2,n}
, see
[
98
, Remark 5.7]
. Theorem
7.13
applies in this situation and yields a new result on this class of cyclic Higgs bundles.

(6)

Non-Hitchin in
\mathsf{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.
In a recent paper

[
17
]
, Bronstein and Davalo considered a special class
of 3-cyclic Higgs bundles for
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
and prove the
corresponding representations are Anosov. By Theorem

7.13
, these cyclic Higgs bundles define
symplectic immersions into the character variety.

(7)

Non-Hitchin in
\mathsf{SL}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{R})
.
In
[
73
]
,
Tamburelli and Rungi gives a correspondence between some

(2n+1)
-cyclic Higgs bundles for
\mathsf{SL}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{R})
and some special
surfaces in the para-complex hyperbolic space. Theorem

7.13
also applies in that case and defines
symplectic immersions into the character variety.

Finally, as a corollary of Theorem
7.13
, we obtain Theorem
H
of the introduction.

Corollary 7.14
.

Let
\mathbf{Y}\subset\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
either be the submanifold Hitchin representations into the split real form when
\mathrm{rk}(\mathsf{G})=2
or the submanifold of maximal
\mathsf{SO}_{2,n}
-representations when
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{SO}_{2+n}\mathbb{C}
.
Let
\mathbf{Z}\subset\mathbf{W}_{0}
be the submanifold of minimal surfaces equivariant with respect to the points of
\mathbf{Y}
.
Then

\mathrm{H}:\mathbf{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Y}

is a global symplectomorphism.
In particular,
h_{0}
defines a mapping class group invariant pseudo-Kähler metric on
\mathbf{Y}
which has signature
\left((\dim\mathsf{G}-3)(g-1),3g-3\right)
and is compatible with the Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbf{M}(\mathsf{G})
be such that
\mathrm{H}(x)
is a maximal representation into
\mathsf{SO}_{2,n}
or a Hitchin representation in rank
2
(hence
x\in\mathbf{M}_{0}
). In that case, the condition
x\in\mathbf{W}(\mathsf{G})
is equivalent to being cyclic of order
3,

4
or
6
when
\mathfrak{g}
is
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}\mathbb{C}
,
\mathfrak{so}_{n+2}\mathbb{C}
or
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
, respectively.
Such cyclic Higgs bundles are described in items
(1)
and
(2)
above, and so we can apply Theorem
7.13
.

Since the dimensions of
\mathbf{W}_{0}
and
\mathbf{X}(\mathsf{G})
coincide,
\mathrm{H}
restricts
to a local symplectomorphism from
\mathbf{Z}
to
\mathbf{Y}
. The proof that

\mathrm{H}
is a global symplectomorphism follows from applying
[
55
, Theorem 8.1.1]
.
∎

Appendix A
Kähler identities and inner products

Here, we summarize the conventions for hermitian geometry on Riemann
surfaces that we use throughout the paper.
Let
X=(\Sigma,j)
be a Riemann surface with its hyperbolic conformal metric,
expressed as

ds^{2}=\rho(dx^{2}+dy^{2})
in local conformal coordinates
z=x+iy
.
We shall refer to the metric simply as
\rho
.
The area (Kähler) form is

\nu_{\rho}:=\tfrac{i}{2}\rho\,dz\wedge d\bar{z}

By definition, the pointwise norms are given by:

|\partial_{x}|_{\rho}^{2}=|\partial_{y}|_{\rho}^{2}=\rho\quad,\quad|dx|_{\rho}^{2}=|dy|_{\rho}^{2}=\rho^{-1}

Hence,
\star dx=dy
,
\star dy=-dx
, so that

dx\wedge\star dx=|dx|_{\rho}^{2}\rho\,dx\wedge dy
,
etc.
Now we have
dz=dx+idy
, and
\star dz=dy-idx=-idz
;

\star d\bar{z}=+id\bar{z}
.
This gives us:

\bar{\star}dz=id\bar{z}\quad,\quad\bar{\star}d\bar{z}=-idz\ .

Notice that
\bar{\star}^{2}=-1
. We also have, of course,

\bar{\star}\nu_{\rho}=1
,
\bar{\star}1=\nu_{\rho}
.
Under the real isomorphism of the tangent space with Riemannian metric and

(1,0)
vectors with the induced hermitian metric,

\partial_{x}\mapsto\partial_{z}
,
\partial_{y}\mapsto i\partial_{z}
,
we have
|\partial_{z}|_{\rho}^{2}=\rho/2
.
Then

dz\wedge\bar{\star}dz=idz\wedge d\bar{z}=|dz|_{\rho}^{2}\,\tfrac{i}{2}\rho\,dz\wedge d\bar{z}=|dz|_{\rho}^{2}\,\nu_{\rho}

We define the
L^{2}
-hermitian inner product on the space
\Omega^{p,q}(X)

of complex valued
(p,q)
-forms by:

(A.1)

\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\rangle:=\int_{X}\alpha_{1}\wedge\bar{\star}\alpha_{2}

More explicitly, we have

(A.2)

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\rangle=i\int_{X}\psi_{1}\wedge\overline{\psi}_{2}\quad,\quad\psi_{i}\in\Omega^{1,0}(X)

(A.3)

\displaystyle\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle=-i\int_{X}\beta_{1}\wedge\overline{\beta}_{2}\quad,\quad\beta_{i}\in\Omega^{0,1}(X)

It follows immediately from (
A.1
)
that the formal adjoint of
\bar{\partial}
with respect to the pairing
(
A.3
) is

\bar{\partial}^{\ast}=-\bar{\star}\bar{\partial}\bar{\star}
.
Define
\Lambda:\Omega^{2}(X)\to\Omega^{0}(X)
by extending

\Lambda(\nu_{\rho})=1
complex linearly. Then we have
the Kähler identities:

(A.4)

\bar{\partial}^{\ast}=-i[\Lambda,\partial]\quad,\quad\partial^{\ast}=-i[\Lambda,\partial]

The hermitian structure on sections of the holomorphic tangent bundle

\Omega^{0}(TX)
is given by:

(A.5)

\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=\int_{X}\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{\rho}\,\nu_{\rho}\quad,\quad v_{i}\in\Omega^{0}(TX)

This is dual to the inner product on
(1,0)
-forms. Indeed,
writing (
A.2
) locally, we see that

\langle\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\rangle=\int_{X}\langle\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\rangle_{\rho}\,\nu_{\rho}\quad,\quad\psi_{i}\in\Omega^{1,0}(X)

Let
q_{k}
be a
k
-differential on
X
, i.e. a section of
K_{X}^{\otimes k}
.
In a local conformal coordinate
z
on
X
, we write
q_{k}=q_{k}(z)dz^{k}
and
define the pointwise norm:

|q_{k}|^{2}_{\rho}(z):=|q_{k}(z)|^{2}(\rho(z)/2)^{-k}

This is independent of the choice of conformal coordinate.

Finally, given a Cartan involution
\tau
, we define a pointwise norm on
Higgs fields as follows. In local conformal coordinates, write

\Phi=\Phi(z)dz
. Then

|\Phi|_{\rho,\tau}^{2}(z):=\kappa_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Phi(z),\Phi^{\ast}(z))(\rho(z)/2)^{-1}\ .

Appendix B
Link with algebraic geometry

For the purposes of this paper, we have not needed the fact that

{\bf M}(\mathsf{G})
is locally isomorphic to
the analytification of the moduli space of stable relative
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles
constructed, for example, in
[
84
]
and

[
37
]
.
This assertion can be shown by producing local universal families on

{\bf M}(\mathsf{G})
and then using the modular properties of the algebraic space.
Such an argument is by now standard, but here we omit the details.
It will be useful, however, to show that the deformation theory agrees, and
that is the purpose of this section.

Fix a smooth complex projective curve
X
and a complex reductive group

\mathsf{G}
.
Let
\pi:\mathcal{P}\to X
be an algebraic principal
\mathsf{G}
-bundle.
Recall that the
Atiyah algebra

{\rm At}(\mathcal{P})
of
\mathcal{P}

is the sheaf (coherent analytic on
X
) of invariant holomorphic vector fields on
\mathcal{P}
.
We have a tautological sequence:

(B.1)

0\longrightarrow\ad(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow{\rm At}(\mathcal{P})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.5359pt\sigma\hskip 8.19447pt}T_{X}\longrightarrow 0

where
\sigma
is projection to
X
.
We have a homomorphism

{\rm At}(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow{\rm At}(\ad(\mathcal{P}))~:~V\longmapsto D_{V}

where
{\rm At}(\ad(\mathcal{P}))

is identified with first order differential operators on
\ad(\mathcal{P})
with
scalar symbol:

\sigma(D_{V})=\sigma(V)
. Explicitly, given
V\in{\rm At}(\mathcal{P})
, and regarding a
local section
s
of
\ad(\mathcal{P})
over
U\subset X
as an equivariant map

s:\pi^{-1}(U)\to\mathfrak{g}
, we define
D_{V}(s):=V(s)
.
We also define a homomorphism

{\rm At}(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow{\rm At}(\ad(\mathcal{P})\otimes K_{X})

(
K_{X}=T^{\ast}_{X}
)
as follows. For local sections
s
of
\ad(\mathcal{P})
and
\alpha
of

K_{X}
, let

D_{V}(s\otimes\alpha):=D_{V}(s)\otimes\alpha+s\otimes d\alpha(\sigma(V))

One checks that this is a well-defined differential operator with

\sigma(D_{V})=\sigma(V)
.
With this understood, we have the following

Lemma B.1

(cf. Welters
[
93
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{P},\Phi)
be a
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle on
X
.
Consider the complex

\mathscr{B}^{\bullet}:{\rm At}(\mathcal{P})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.5359pt\ad_{\Phi}\hskip 8.19447pt}\ad(\mathcal{P})\otimes K_{X}

where
\ad_{\Phi}(V):=[\Phi,D_{V}]
.
Then the hypercohomology
\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathscr{B}^{\bullet})
parametrizes first
order joint deformations of
(X,\mathcal{P},\Phi)
.

Next, let
P
denote the underlying smooth
\mathsf{G}
-bundle, and
J\in\mathcal{J}(P)

such that
\mathcal{P}\simeq(P,J)
. Let
\bar{\partial}_{\ad(P)}
denote the

\bar{\partial}
-operator on
\ad(P)
defining the holomorphic bundle
\ad(\mathcal{P})
.
We have the following
C^{\infty}
resolution of
\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}
:

(B.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm At}(\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\aut(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad(\mathcal{P})\otimes K_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{1,0}(X,\ad(P))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{1,1}(X,\ad(P))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad_{\Phi}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad_{\Phi}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad_{\Phi}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 8.19447pt\bar{\partial}_{\ad(P)}\hskip 8.19447pt}

Here, the map
L
is given by
V\mapsto L_{V}J
, for a smooth invariant
vector field
V
on
P
. At a solution to the Hitchin equations,
the diagram commutes if we define
\ad_{\Phi}
on
T_{J}\mathcal{J}(P)
by:

(\mu,\beta)\mapsto-\tfrac{1}{2i}(\partial_{A}(\Phi\mu)+[\Phi,\beta])

Hence, the complex
B^{\bullet}
from §
4.5
gives a smooth resolution of

\mathscr{B}^{\bullet}
.
The next result is an immediate consequence.

Proposition B.2
.

We have an isomorphism:

\mathbb{H}^{i}\left({\rm At}(\mathscr{B}^{\bullet})\right)\simeq H^{i}(B^{\bullet})
,

i=0,1,2
.

Appendix C
The algebraic definition of
\Theta

In this section we give the algebro-geometric definition of
\Theta
(see
also
[
20
]
).
Continuing with the notation of the previous section, let

\mathscr{A}^{\bullet}
denote the complex

\mathscr{A}^{\bullet}:\ad(\mathcal{P})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.5359pt\ad_{\Phi}\hskip 8.19447pt}\ad(\mathcal{P})\otimes K_{X}

Then
\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathscr{A}^{\bullet})
parameterizes the tangent space to

{\bf M}_{X}(\mathsf{G})
at a stable Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
on
X

[
70
]
.
The exact sequence of complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad(\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm At}(\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad(\mathcal{P})\otimes K_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad(\mathcal{P})\otimes K_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad_{\Phi}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad_{\Phi}\hskip 8.19447pt}

gives the tangent sequence of
{\bf M}(\mathsf{G})\to{\bf T}(\Sigma)
at a stable
Higgs bundle:

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathscr{A}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathscr{B}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow H^{1}(T_{X})\longrightarrow 0

On the other hand, notice that
\Phi
defines a map of complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad(\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad(\mathcal{P})\otimes K_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{2i}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ad_{\Phi}\hskip 8.19447pt}

since
\Phi
commutes with itself. Let
\Theta^{alg}
be the induced map

H^{1}(T_{X})\to\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathscr{A}^{\bullet})
. The following is then
straightforward.

Proposition C.1
.

In terms of the isomorphism from Proposition
B.2
,
the holomorphic section
\Theta^{alg}
defined above
satisfies

\Theta^{alg}([\mu])=[(0,\frac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu,0)]
for all
[\mu]\in H^{1}(T_{X})
.

Appendix D
Comparison with the formula of Tošić

We explain here why our formula (
1.1
) for the complex Hessian of the energy agrees
with the one found by Tošić in
[
90
, Theorem 1.10]
.
Fix a Beltrami differential
\mu
and a harmonic bundle
(J,\Phi)
. For
simplicity, set
(\mu,\beta,\psi)
to be the minimal norm (horizontal)
tangent vector, and let
(\mu,\beta_{1},\psi_{1})
,
(i\mu,\beta_{2},\psi_{2})
be
the isomonodromic tangent vectors. Recall that from Lemma
5.13
,

\beta=\tfrac{1}{2}(\beta_{1}-i\beta_{2})\quad,\quad\psi=\tfrac{1}{2}(\psi_{1}-i\psi_{2})

and from Lemma
5.5
,
\|(\beta_{i},\psi_{i})\|=\|\Phi\mu/2i\|
for
i=1,2
.
Finally, we have also proven that (see Lemma
5.12
)

(D.1)

\bar{\partial}_{A}\zeta_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2i}(-\psi_{i}^{\ast}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)\quad,\quad[(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)^{\ast},\zeta_{i}]=\tfrac{1}{2i}\beta_{i}

for
i=1,2
.

Now there are two differences in the normalizations used here compared
with those in
[
90
]
. First, our
derivatives are computed with respect to
\mu
, whereas in

[
90
]
they are computed
with respect to
\tfrac{1}{2i}\mu
. This means that the (complex) variation of the harmonic
metric, which Tošić calls
w
, is
2i
times the variation that we obtain.
So,

(D.2)

w=2i\times\tfrac{1}{2}(\zeta_{1}-i\zeta_{2})=i\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{2}

The second difference is that the Higgs field, which Tošić calls
\partial f_{0}
,
corresponds to our
\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi
. Hence, in the notation of

[
90
, Theorem 1.10]
,

(D.3)

\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu=\bar{\partial}_{A}\varphi+\theta

where
\theta\perp\imag\bar{\partial}_{A}
.

With this understood, we proceed to calculate Tošić’s expression for
the Hessian
\Delta_{\mu}E
:

(D.4)

\Delta_{\mu}E=8\|\theta\|^{2}+8\|\bar{\partial}_{A}(w-\varphi)\|^{2}-8i\int_{X}\langle R(w,\partial f_{0}\wedge\bar{\partial}f_{0},w\rangle

where
R
is the curvature of the (appropriately normalized) invariant
metric on
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{K}
.
Using (
D.2
) and (
D.1
), we have

\bar{\partial}_{A}w=-\tfrac{1}{2i}(\psi_{1}+i\psi_{2})^{\ast}+\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu

Now from (
D.3
) and the fact that
\theta\perp\imag\bar{\partial}_{A}
,

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{A}(w-\varphi)

\displaystyle=\bar{\partial}_{A}w+\theta-\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu

\displaystyle\|\bar{\partial}_{A}(w-\varphi)\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\bar{\partial}_{A}w\|^{2}+\|\theta\|^{2}+\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}-2\Real\langle\bar{\partial}_{A}w+\theta,\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\rangle

But
\tfrac{1}{2i}\langle\theta,\Phi\mu\rangle=\|\theta\|^{2}
, so

\|\bar{\partial}_{A}(w-\varphi)\|^{2}=\|\bar{\partial}_{A}w\|^{2}-\|\theta\|^{2}+\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}-2\Real\langle\bar{\partial}_{A}w,\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\rangle

which we can write as

\|\theta\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}_{A}(w-\varphi)\|^{2}=\|\bar{\partial}_{A}w-\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}(\|\psi_{1}\|^{2}+\|\psi_{2}\|^{2}+2\Real\langle\psi_{1},i\psi_{2}\rangle)

For the last term in (
D.4
), we have

\displaystyle-i\int_{X}\langle R(w,\partial f_{0}\wedge\bar{\partial}f_{0},w\rangle

\displaystyle=\|[\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu,i\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{2}]\|^{2}=\|[(\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu)^{\ast},-i\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{2}]\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\tfrac{1}{2}(\beta_{1}+i\beta_{2})\|^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}(\|\beta_{1}\|^{2}+\|\beta_{2}\|^{2}+2\Real\langle\beta_{1},i\beta_{2}\rangle)

It follows that

(D.5)

\Delta_{\mu}E=4(\|\Phi\mu/2i\|^{2}+\Real\langle(\beta_{1},\psi_{1}),(i\beta_{2},i\psi_{2})\rangle)

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\|(\beta,\psi)\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4}(\|(\beta_{1},\psi_{1})\|^{2}+\|(\beta_{1},\psi_{1})\|^{2}-2\Real\langle\beta_{1},i\beta_{2}\rangle)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}-\Real\langle(\beta_{1},\psi_{1}),(i\beta_{2},i\psi_{2})\rangle)

\displaystyle\Real\langle(\beta_{1},\psi_{1}),(i\beta_{2},i\psi_{2})\rangle)

\displaystyle=\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}-2\|(\beta,\psi)\|^{2}

Finally, plugging this into (
D.5
), we obtain

\Delta_{\mu}E=8(\|\tfrac{1}{2i}\Phi\mu\|^{2}-\|(\beta,\psi)\|^{2})=-8\|w_{\mu}\|^{2}_{h_{0}}\ ,

which agrees with (
1.1
) (see also Lemma
5.9
).
